⏺ The Status and Roadmap sections on www.s4ql.com are updated and live.

  - Status: "Built" now has a line saying the shape of the syntax tree is
    checked against SQLite, with the same EXPLAIN output and the same values,
    from PR #26. "Not built" now starts with the pass after the parser. The date
    (16 November) and "Next" were still correct, so they're unchanged.
  - Roadmap: the parser moved to Built. Its text now says what exists: it agrees
    with SQLite on which statements parse, on the message of each syntax error,
    and on the shape of the syntax tree. A new item, "The pass after the
    parser", is marked "Agreed, not built", taken from docs/todo.md.
  - README: its Status line now also mentions the shape check, so it matches the
    site.

✻ Churned for 7s · done Wednesday 1:13 PM

❯ Great.                                                                        
                                                                                
  I'd like to go back to the simulator. I'd like to make it work with a SQLite  
  WebAssembly build +  a WebAssembly build of our Go code, but with no changes  
  to the Go codebase otherwise. Can we do that?                                 

  Searched for 2 patterns, listed 3 directories (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Yes, with one exception. internal/sqlite is a cgo binding, and cgo doesn't
  build for the browser, so that one package needs a second version of its API
  for the browser. No other Go code has to change. The research already showed
  this works: internal/core, its tests and its simulation ran in Chromium on the
  official SQLite WebAssembly build. 208 of 211 subtests passed, and the
  simulation traces were identical to the native ones.

  What we'd add (no existing Go file is edited):
  1. A browser version of internal/sqlite and of internal/sqlite/faultvfs. In
     the research they were 304 lines of Go plus about 200 lines of JavaScript
     (a VFS, the layer through which SQLite reads and writes files). Go's
     -overlay flag can inject them at build time, so the repository's Go tree
     stays exactly as it is. They could also live in the repo as files that only
     build for the browser.
  2. A small Go entry point, built for the browser, that runs internal/core
     against the simulated S3 store with the real simulator and passes the
     traces to the page. It can sit outside the shipped packages, for example
     next to the site.
  3. The page, which runs it in a Worker and shows the traces in place of
     today's JavaScript model.

  What the page could and couldn't do:
  - Faults and seeds: these work, through the simulated store's existing
    settings and the existing simulator. Same seed, same run, same trace as go 
    test natively.
  - The four planted bugs: they can only be switched on from internal/core's own
    tests, because nothing else in Go can reach them. Two ways around that,
    neither changing existing code:
    - Run the real test binary in the page, as the research did. That's
      faithful, but you'd pick a workload and seed and watch it run, with no
      clicking in faults live.
    - Have the overlay add one more file inside internal/core that turns them
      on. That's interactive, but the build then contains code the repo doesn't.
  - Fake time: the simulator only runs inside Go's testing/synctest, which needs
    a test context. The entry point would start through Go's testing package to
    get one. It's a bit unusual, but nothing changes.
  - Download size: probably 10 to 25 MB, about 4 MB compressed. Today's
    JavaScript model is a few KB.

  Two questions for you:
  1. Which SQLite? The official build is the release SQLite's developers ship,
     not our pinned version with our compile options. Building our own pinned
     copy of SQLite with Emscripten is more work, but it would be the same
     SQLite as the cgo build.
  2. Should the browser code live in the repo? If yes, the spec's rule that the
     binding is our own cgo binding needs a sentence for the browser target.
     That wording is yours to agree. With the overlay, the binding the module
     ships doesn't change at all.

  I'd go with the official build and the overlay to start, and run the test
  binary first, since that's the most faithful version. Interactive fault
  injection would come second. Should I hand that to a builder?

✻ Sautéed for 55s · done Wednesday 1:15 PM

❯ /fabrik:one-at-a-time                                                         

⏺ Yes, with one exception. internal/sqlite calls SQLite through cgo, and cgo
  doesn't build for the browser. That one package needs a second version of its
  API, written for the browser. No other Go code has to change.

  The research already showed this works. internal/core, its tests and its
  simulation ran in Chromium on the official SQLite WebAssembly build. 208 of
  211 subtests passed, and the simulation traces matched the native ones
  exactly.

✻ Worked for 6s · done Wednesday 1:19 PM

❯ What's an overlay?                                                            

⏺ An overlay is a JSON file you pass to go build -overlay=overlay.json. It maps
  paths in the source tree to files somewhere else. The Go tools then build as
  if those files were in the tree, without changing anything on disk.

  {
    "Replace": {
      "/repo/internal/sqlite/sqlite_js.go":
  "/repo/site/simulator/wasm/sqlite_js.go"
    }
  }

  During that build, internal/sqlite has an extra file, sqlite_js.go, even
  though the repo's internal/sqlite doesn't contain it. The cgo files drop out
  of the browser build by themselves, because cgo is off for that target. The
  overlay can also blank out an existing file by mapping its path to "", but
  here it only needs to add files.

  So the browser code would sit outside internal/, for example next to the
  simulator. It only takes effect in the one build command that produces the
  WebAssembly file. go build, go test and anyone importing maragu.dev/s4ql never
  see it.

  The downside is that the code isn't type-checked, and doesn't show up in
  editors or go vet, unless you build with the overlay. The site's build step
  would run it and catch breakage there.

  Overlay or files in the repo?

✻ Cogitated for 7s · done Wednesday 1:20 PM

❯ Isn't that what build tags are for?                                           

⏺ Yes, it is. A file named sqlite_js.go in internal/sqlite only builds for the
  browser target (GOOS=js), and the cgo files are left out of that build
  automatically. No tag is even needed, because the _js suffix does the job.
  That's the usual way to do it, and editors, go vet and CI all see the file.

  I suggested the overlay only because you said no changes to the Go codebase.
  Build-tagged files are new files in internal/sqlite. They change nothing that
  exists today, but they are in the codebase, and the spec rule about our own
  cgo binding would need a sentence for the browser target.

  If new files count as fine, I'd use _js.go files over the overlay. Is that
  fine?

✻ Crunched for 8s · done Wednesday 1:20 PM

❯ Ah, now I understand. So if library users also want to build for webassembly, 
  they'd use the same files with the build tags? That's arguably a feature.     
  Alternatively, we invent our own build tag just for the browser simulator.    

⏺ Yes. With a plain _js.go file, anyone who builds s4ql for GOOS=js gets that
  version, so s4ql would run in the browser for library users too. The S3 client
  works there as well, since Go's HTTP client goes through the browser's fetch,
  as long as the bucket allows the page's origin (CORS).

  It makes browser support a promise, though, and it would be a rough one today:
  - SQLite is separate. The official build is a JavaScript module the page has
    to load next to the Go WebAssembly file. A user can't just go build and have
    it work.
  - Three tests fail there, and their causes are in the research note.
  - The SQLite isn't pinned. It's the official release, not our pinned version
    with our compile options.

  With a tag of our own, say s4ql_browser, only the simulator's build turns it
  on, and nobody gets it by accident. Promoting it to a feature later just means
  dropping the tag, once the three points above are dealt with.

  I'd start with our own tag and treat browser support for users as a separate
  product decision. Own tag?

✻ Worked for 10s · done Wednesday 1:21 PM

❯ Own tag                                                                       

⏺ Our own tag it is, s4ql_browser. The spec rule about our cgo binding may still
  want a sentence about it, and I'll bring you wording once the code exists.

    release, built with their options rather than ours.
  - Our pinned copy, built with Emscripten, a compiler from C to WebAssembly. It
    would use the same SQLite source and options.h as the cgo build, so the
    page runs the same SQLite as the tests do natively. This hasn't been tried.
    It needs Emscripten installed, and we'd own a build step for it.

  our pinned copy would be a later step, and only if the differences in version

    code in core. But it isn't interactive: no live fault injection, no

✻ Crunched for 5s · done Wednesday 1:23 PM

❯ Would we need to change the core otherwise? Or keep it up to date?            

⏺ Neither route changes core beyond what I've described.

  Test binary: no change to core, and nothing in it to keep up to date. The page
  runs the tests as they are, so a rebuild picks up whatever the tests are at
  that point. The only coupling is the list of workload and test names the page
  offers, and the build can read that from the binary itself (-test.list).

  Interactive: one file in core, behind the tag, that sets the fields of bugs.
  When a planted bug is added or renamed, that file has to follow. The entry
  point also uses the APIs of core, simstore and sim, so it has to follow
  changes to those.

  Both routes: the browser version of internal/sqlite and faultvfs must keep the
  same API as the cgo binding. When the binding gains a function, the browser
  version needs it too. That's the main thing to keep up to date.

  What catches drift is a CI job that builds with s4ql_browser for GOOS=js. It
  fails at compile time on any mismatch, which is cheap and tells the builder
  what to fix. Running the tests in a browser in CI would also catch differences
  in behaviour, but it's slow, taking 3 to 5 minutes in the research.

  Test binary first?
✻ Cooked for 11s · done Wednesday 1:24 PM

❯ Wouldn't it be way cooler to be able to inject arbitrary bugs in the browser? 

⏺ Yes, it would be. "Arbitrary" has a limit, though, depending on what's being
  injected:

  - Faults in the environment can be arbitrary and live. S3 losing, delaying or
    reordering requests, instances crashing, disk writes failing, clocks
    drifting. These are all set at run time, through the simulated store,
    faultvfs and the simulator.
  - Bugs in the code are limited to the planted ones. There are eight today in
    bugs, and you'd pick them before a run. A bug nobody wrote in advance would
    mean compiling Go in the browser.

  The interactive version gives you both, with faults injected while it runs and
  planted bugs chosen at the start. Skipping the test-binary step costs little.
  I'd go straight to interactive, and the CI build with s4ql_browser keeps it
  from drifting. Interactive?

⏺ That makes it simpler. No file in core and no planted bugs: the page runs the

  Next point: time. The simulator only runs inside Go's testing/synctest, which
  gives it fake time and a deterministic order. That's how a seed replays
  exactly. synctest needs a test context, so the entry point would start through
  Go's testing package (testing.MainStart) and run the simulation as a test
  inside the page. It's an unusual way to start a program, but it's supported,
  and it needs no change to sim.

  With that in place, the same seed and the same faults give the same trace
  every time, so a run can be shared by URL as the simulator does today. Fine?

  ## Decisions you should check

  1. **`BROWSERSKIP`.** Two canaries in `internal/sim/cgo_test.go`
  (`TestSynctestWithCgo/...among_parallel_readers...`) cannot pass in a browser.
     - Each runs 8 readers in a loop that never blocks. Go's js/wasm port has
  one thread and no preemption, so the first reader runs alone forever, and the
  test hung in Chromium.
     - I could not edit `internal/sim`, so `make test-browser` passes `-skip
  TestSynctestWithCgo/among_parallel_readers`. The measured cause is written
  next to it in the Makefile.
     - If you prefer, a skip under the tag inside `internal/sim` would be
  cleaner, but that edits sim.
  2. **`internal/simlint/simlint.go`.** The runner and the new update tool are
  added to its exempt list, the same way `internal/sqlite/update` is. Without
  this the simulation lint fails `make lint` on the runner's goroutines and
  channel waits.
  3. **The route's own limits, documented in the README:**
     - While SQLite sleeps in its busy handler, other goroutines run. But the
  call returns only once every goroutine is idle, so a goroutine that never
  waits keeps SQLite asleep.
     - A file operation of Go's `os` cannot finish while Go is inside a call
  into SQLite.
  4. **Hash attribution in the research note is off.** Its §7 pairs the hashes
  with the wrong workloads; with `-v` the lines of parallel subtests interleave.
  Read with `go test -json` on both sides:

  | Workload | Native and Chromium |
  |---|---|
  | one instance |
  `5367e1c27016f858a1fbccc2bdfe663b79de8e5d1f924fb3d343243cdde27438` |
  | writes that land long after they were sent |
  `474a8a3e319f873ac7412eea68a48056729b88992066712476a8d3f51d0bb46c` |
  | instances that compete, clocks that move, crashes, buggify |
  `9e40067391d1eab7f74b34fbfb9060a6e6f9c5735742314337ed33fa7b7e7809` |
  | callers that never pause |
  `a063e0fc51242c14b6a5ff5b11ce7ff4d7cd10a17a33beb974b88f96539b03c7` |

  The four hashes themselves match the research's.

  ## What I added, and where

  - **`/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.claude/worktrees/agent-a6e489eadd3f68d9d
  /internal/sqlite/sqlite_browser.go`** (`//go:build js && wasm &&
  s4ql_browser`):
    - The API of the cgo binding that core, its tests, `faultvfs` and the sim
  canaries use. It calls the build's exported C functions through `syscall/js`.
    - All bytes pass through one transfer `Uint8Array`. A view per call filled
  `syscall/js`'s value table, and the test crashed with `RangeError: Map maximum
  size exceeded`.
    - 64-bit integers pass as Numbers when exact, and as decimal text otherwise.
    - Left out: `Tokenize`, `DenyProcessPragmas`, `AddTestFunctions`, `FindVFS`,
  `RegisterVFS`, `UnregisterVFS`.
  - **Making up for the official build's options:**
    - Every connection runs PRAGMAs on open: page size 4096, page cache -2000
  for main and temp, foreign keys on, temp store file.
    - `Stmt.Step` keeps the `SQLITE_OMIT_AUTORESET` rule itself.
    - `Close` refuses with `SQLITE_BUSY` like `sqlite3_close`, which the build
  does not export.
    - A native copy of the browser tests agreed with these everywhere except the
  three intended differences below.
  - **`.../internal/sqlite/faultvfs/faultvfs_browser.go`**: the same API, over a
  JS fault state. The unchanged `faultvfs_test.go` passes against it in
  Chromium.
  - **`.../internal/sqlite/sqlite_browser_test.go`**: tests of the bridge:
  integers around 2^53 and at the extremes, text with NUL and non-UTF-8 bytes,
  `Close` with an open statement, stepping a finished or failed statement, the
  PRAGMAs, the page size in the file header, WAL with two connections, files
  visible to `os`, read-only files, and a lock released by another goroutine
  during SQLite's busy wait.
  - **`.../internal/sqlite/browser/`**:
    - `sqlite3.js` and `sqlite3.wasm`, unchanged from
  https://sqlite.org/2026/sqlite-wasm-3540100.zip (728,897 bytes, SHA3-256
  `87e24f6a4588692310b7942dc2fa7c7275bb9ba2e01d553e4115aa4a904a0050`), recorded
  in `source.json`.
    - `update/` vendors and verifies the build (`make sqlite-browser-update
  VERSION=x.y.z`, `make sqlite-browser-verify`; the second also runs in
  `sqlite-verify.yml`).
    - `vfs.js`: the VFS `s4ql`, registered as SQLite's default. Its files are
  those of `fs.js`. File locks follow the unix VFS's rules within one process,
  and the wal-index is in SQLite's heap, shared by every connection. It reports
  power-safe overwrite and 4096-byte sectors like the unix VFS. Its `xSleep`
  calls into Go so other goroutines run, and no method lets an exception reach
  SQLite's C code.
    - `fs.js`: the in-memory file system for Go's `os`, with permission bits
  that bind as for a non-root user. Because of them, the two spool-file tests in
  core that skipped in the first run now run and pass.
    - `bridge.js`, plus `worker.js` and `index.html` for the runner's page.
    - `main.go`: a runner for `go test -exec` that serves the page on 127.0.0.1,
  starts headless Chromium, streams the output back, and exits with the
  program's exit code.
    - `README.md`, drafted by a writer sub-agent and checked by me.
  - **One build constraint on an existing file:** `//go:build !s4ql_browser` on
  `internal/sqlite/sqlite_test.go`. Those are the cgo binding's tests; they use
  functions the browser binding lacks and build the sqlite3 shell.

  ## Differences that remain on purpose

  - Opening reads the schema (the cache_size PRAGMA needs it). So a file that is
  not a database fails at `Open`, and through `faultvfs` an armed fault also
  counts the reads of the open.
  - `pragma temp_store` reads 1 instead of 0.
  - The build has `THREADSAFE=0`, `OMIT_UTF16`, `OMIT_DEPRECATED`, `USE_URI` and
  extra extensions, and the VFS is ours, not the unix VFS.

  |---|---|---|
  | corrupt cache | page size 8192 | the PRAGMAs |

  | lock held for a while | SQLite's busy handler blocked the only thread |
  `xSleep` calls into Go so the holder can release the lock |

  ## What CI does now

  A new job `browser` in `.github/workflows/ci.yml`:
  - runs `make check-browser`: build, vet and `go test -c` of every browser
  package for js/wasm with the tag, which fails on any drift from the cgo
  binding's API;
  - runs golangci-lint over the browser build (`golangci-lint-action` with
  `GOOS=js GOARCH=wasm`);
  - runs `make test-browser` in the runner image's Chrome with
  `S4QL_CHROMIUM_FLAGS=--no-sandbox`.

  I included the test run because it was reliable locally and takes about 3
  minutes. It assumes `google-chrome` is on the PATH of `ubuntu-latest`; drop
  that step if it proves flaky. `make lint` also lints the browser build
  locally.

  ## Self-review

  The code-review skill at medium gave ten findings.
  - **Fixed:** exceptions unwinding through SQLite's C frames; read-only files
  opened for writing; the lock rule between connections; leaked fault VFS
  structs; dead fallback code in `vfs.js`.
  - **Documented:** the schema read at open, and `temp_store`.
  - **Rejected:** a DQS difference (the official build has `DQS=0`, like
  `options.h`), and sharing a `Conn` between goroutines (not supported by either
  binding).
  - **Not done:** moving the code that does not depend on C into files shared by
  both bindings, because that would edit `sqlite.go` and `faultvfs.go`.

  dd3f68d9d/docs/diary/2026-11-18-browser-sqlite-binding.md` has three steps,
  with the exact errors.

  ## What step 2 depends on

  - A page runs the Go program in a Worker that loads, in order, `sqlite3.js`,
  `vfs.js`, `bridge.js`, `fs.js`, and the `wasm_exec.js` of the GOROOT that
  built the program.
  - It awaits `s4qlInitSQLite()`, then runs the module with `new Go()`.
  `globalThis.s4qlOutput(fd, bytes)` is optional and receives standard output
  and error. `worker.js` is the example.
  - Serving with COOP `same-origin` and COEP `require-corp` lets the VFS wait
  with `Atomics.wait` instead of spinning.
  - The program's working directory is the root of `fs.js`, which holds only
  `/tmp`.
  - If the page is too slow, the research timed `//go:wasmimport` at 6 ns a call
  against 807 ns through `syscall/js`.
  - Follow-up: a CI check that the browser's trace hashes equal the native ones;
  today that is a manual comparison.

  What step 1 delivered
  - The real core runs in Chromium on the official SQLite 3.54.1 build, behind
    s4ql_browser. internal/core passes 44 tests and 213 subtests there with no
    failures. The three failures from the research were fixed, not skipped.
  - The traces match. I re-ran the simulation natively and in Chromium myself,
    and the four trace hashes are identical.
  - CI has a new browser job that builds with the tag and runs the tests in
    headless Chrome, so the browser binding can't drift from the cgo one
    unnoticed. It passed in 6 min 40 s.
  - Nothing in core, sim, simstore or the spec changed. Two existing Go files
    were touched: a build constraint on the cgo binding's test file, and two
    entries in the simulation lint's exempt list.
  - Two sim tests are skipped in the browser. They check for parallel readers,
    and Go in a browser has one thread, so they hang there.
  - Sizes: SQLite is 0.5 MB compressed, and the core test binary is 3.3 MB
    compressed.

  Two things I decided for step 2
  - I split it in two. The builder running now writes the engine: the Go program
    the page talks to. A second one will build the page and the deploy after
    that.
  - Injecting a fault re-runs the simulation. The page sends the seed plus the
    list of faults with their fake times, and Go runs the whole thing and
    returns the trace. When you pause and add a fault, the page asks for the run
    again, and since runs are deterministic everything before that moment is
    identical. A live loop would let fake time run on while the simulation waits
    for your click, and leases would expire while you think. The re-run design
    avoids that, and it makes the shareable URL exact.

› Message from fabrik:builder
  The engine is built and works end to end in Chromium. `make lint`, `make
  test`, `make check-browser` and `make test-browser` all pass on the last
  commit. There is one gap: file-system faults are not reachable without a
  change to `core`, which the brief rules out, so that is your call (see
  Questions for you).

  **Branch:** `worktree-agent-a5a0078322cb99fba`, seven commits on `main`:
  - 9abdfe1, 2fe7925: the engine and the program
  - b1e5d38: tests, Makefile and CI
  - bb3508b, f59721d: diary steps 2 and 3
  - d40f640, d305eef: fixes from the review and from the README writer

  Diary: `docs/diary/2026-11-18-browser-simulator-engine.md`. Nothing is pushed.

  ## Where it lives
  - `internal/simulator`: the engine as a library, tagged `s4ql_browser`. It
  builds natively (cgo SQLite) and for `js/wasm`, so `simlint` covers it and its
  tests run in both. Files: `request.go`, `trace.go`, `run.go`, `check.go`,
  `state.go`, `simulator.go`, and `README.md` (the spec for 2b).
  - `internal/simulator/engine`: the program for the browser.
    - It is the test binary of this package (`go test -c`), whose one test,
  `TestEngine`, serves the page's messages. That is how it gets a `*testing.T`
  without implementing `testing.MainStart`'s `testDeps`.
    - It also holds `worker.js`, `simulator.js` (the ES module a page imports)
  and `index.html` (the demo).
  - `internal/simulator/serve`: a native command that builds `engine.wasm`,
  copies everything a page needs into one directory, and optionally serves it.
  `make simulator SIMULATORDIR=dir` and `make simulator-demo`
  (http://127.0.0.1:8080/) run it.

  ## Design
  I kept your run-to-completion design: one request is one `synctest` bubble on
  `sim.New(seed)`, and the response is the whole trace.
  - **Prefix property:** schedules that agree strictly before T give identical
  events before T, and a later `end` does not change earlier events. Both are
  tested.
  - **No change to `simstore`:** it traces through a wrapper of the scheduler.
  Each trace line becomes a structured event by its format string. A record in
  the caller's context ties every line to its call, because `sim` passes context
  values on to the store's carrier tasks.
  - **Core-side data:** the lease holder ID and buggify firings come from a
  wrapper of `sim.Instance` given to `core.Open`. Core events come from
  `Options.Logger`.
  - **No snapshots during a run:** opening a cache or waking a task would change
  the run. The state at T is folded from the events afterwards (`StateAt`, or
  the page folds them itself).

  ## Request and trace format (detail in the README)
  - **Request:**
    - Fields: `seed`, `end`, `instances` (1 to 5), `leases`, `readers`,
  `latency`, `faults`, `buggify`, `workload` (on, callers, think, writes,
  timeout), `schedule`, `lines`.
    - Every time is in fake milliseconds from the start of the run.
    - `faults` holds the `simstore.Faults` probabilities and durations, plus
  `damaged`.
  - **Actions:** write, read, crash, restart (new process `a2` on a copy of the
  crashed cache), close, open, cutOff, outage, calm, shiftWall, stopWall,
  startWall, stopMonotonic, startMonotonic, loseAnswer and failRequest
  (one-shot, matched by purpose and optional instance), and workload on/off.
  - **Workload:** one table `t(k)`. Every write inserts a unique key, so the
  keys tell transactions apart.
  - **Response:** `request` (with defaults filled in), `events`, `summary`,
  `hash` (the same trace hash the core tests compute), `steps`, optional
  `lines`, and `schedulerError`. A refused request gets only `error`.
  - **Event kinds:** process, clock, core, call, request, store, action, check,
  buggify.
    - Requests carry a `purpose` (read, take, commit, renew, release, upload,
  download).
    - A request's life runs sent, slow, held or stuck, arrived, applied or
  refused or read, then answered or lost, with caller-side events alongside.
  - **State at T** (`{"kind":"state","t":T}`):
    - the bucket: manifest, commits, and the log chain;
    - each instance: process, state, holder, lease held or none, cache position,
  wall and monotonic clocks, cut-off until;
    - requests in flight, with phase and whether the caller waits;
    - calls in progress, outage, calm, and the violation count.

  ## Against the old page
  - **Real code cannot do:**
    - stall and resume a process;
    - clocks at a different rate, or reset to true;
    - fail the disk (see the question below);
    - planted bugs (excluded on purpose);
    - switch slow S3 or buggify at a time, since `simstore` takes its faults at
  the start and buggify is per run;
    - reconnect early;
    - check inside caches.
  - **Heal-all and random process chaos** are not engine actions, but a page can
  compose them into the schedule.
  - **Only the engine has:**
    - the real core and SQLite, Protocol Buffers commit objects, and the real
  timeouts;
    - outages and broken reads;
    - conflicts only while another write is in flight;
    - late and held writes up to 30 minutes;
    - 1 to 5 instances, with a lease duration each;
    - the core's own events and buggify;
    - hashes that match the native build.

  ## Invariants
  I wrote a checker in `check.go`. It works over what the calls saw and what the
  store holds, with six invariants:
  - `chain`: the head only moves to a commit whose parent is the old head.
  - `acknowledged`: an acknowledged commit is in the log once, at ack and at the
  end.
  - `not_committed`: a failed write is not in the log at the end.
  - `once`: no key is in the log twice.
  - `state`: a read sees a state the log had at its position.
  - `fresh`: a read sees every commit acknowledged before it started, and every
  unknown-outcome commit that returned before it started and is in the log at
  the end.

  A `fresh` violation is marked excused when the read's process had its
  monotonic clock stopped and its wall clock stopped or set back, a case the
  design accepts. The checker sees nothing inside the core. It can only be
  tested on synthetic input (`check_internal_test.go`) because planted bugs are
  excluded. Ten seeds with every fault and buggify gave no violations.

  ## Tests and results
  `make test-browser` passed on every package, but those times are inflated:
  another session was running a fuzz test with `-parallel 8` alongside, so core
  took 957 s and simstore 924 s. I reran the simulator packages in Chromium on
  the final commit: `internal/simulator` passed in 6.3 s. `go test -race -tags
  s4ql_browser ./internal/simulator/...` passes too.

  The tests show:
  - the same bytes for the same request, twice and on a second engine;
  - ten seeds with every fault, same hash and events twice each;
  - the same events before the point where two schedules differ (at 40000 and at
  61234.5), and before the earlier of two ends;
  - a malformed request, an unknown kind, or a bad state message gets an error
  and the engine goes on;
  - every action has its effect;
  - each store fault and buggify shows its events, and every store line is
  classified;
  - the cache position folded from core events alone matches what each read saw;
  - the checker catches each invariant broken on synthetic input.

  Nothing depends on real time.

  ## Numbers
  Measured on an M4 with headless Chrome:
  - **Size:** `engine.wasm` is 12,123,036 bytes (2,945,531 gzip -9, 2,098,722
  Brotli). With the SQLite build and JS, a page downloads about 3.5 MB gzipped.
  Stripping symbols saves only 2%.
  - **Engine ready:** 100 ms after the page loads the module in a fresh profile,
  measured on 127.0.0.1 where the download costs nothing.
  - **One run** (3 instances, default workload):

  | Run | Time |
  | --- | --- |
  | 2 min | 93 to 110 ms |
  | 2 min, demo request with every fault and six actions | 88 to 110 ms (166 to
  186 ms the first time) |
  | 5 min with a crash | 195 ms |
  | 10 min | 425 ms |
  | 30 min, 5 instances, buggify | 1.47 s, with 6.6 MB of JSON |

  - **Speed:** the browser is about 2.3 times slower than native. Time grows
  with scheduler steps; a native profile puts half of it in the scheduler baton
  and a tenth in spool files. If the page needs more speed: a shorter `end`,
  fewer instances or callers, a longer `think`. `//go:wasmimport` was not
  needed.
  - **Memory:**
    - SQLite's WebAssembly memory grows about 0.55 MB per crashed process,
  because nothing closes a crashed process's connections. It does not grow
  without crashes.
    - `simulator.js` starts a fresh Worker before a run once memory passes 64
  MiB.
    - The official build has no `sqlite3_memory_used`, so it reports the memory
  size.
  - **Hashes:** two requests gave the same hash natively, in the runner and in
  the demo page (`95308c33…`, `b82b8ab6…`). No test compares the two builds.

  ## Changes to existing files
  - `Makefile`:
    - `BROWSERPKGS` gets the two packages, and there is a new `SIMULATORPKGS`;
    - `make test` also runs the simulator natively with the tag, adding about 2
  s;
    - `make lint` adds native golangci-lint with the tag, and `simlint` with
  `GOFLAGS=-tags=s4ql_browser`, because its `-tags` flag has no effect;
    - new `simulator` and `simulator-demo` targets.
  - `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, job `browser`:
    - the js lint covers the two packages;
    - new steps for a native race test, a native lint, and simlint of the
  simulator.
  - No changes to core, sim, simstore, simlint, the spec, the README or `site/`.

  ## For the 2b builder
  - **Files:** serve from one directory on the page's origin: `engine.wasm`,
  `worker.js`, `simulator.js`, `sqlite3.js`, `sqlite3.wasm`, `vfs.js`,
  `bridge.js`, `fs.js`, and `wasm_exec.js` from the same Go that built the
  engine. Serve `engine.wasm` as `application/wasm`. COOP/COEP headers are
  optional; without them the VFS spins.
  - **Build:** `make simulator SIMULATORDIR=dir`, which runs `GOOS=js
  GOARCH=wasm go test -c -tags s4ql_browser -o dir/engine.wasm
  ./internal/simulator/engine` and copies the rest.
  - **Load order** is handled by `worker.js`: sqlite3.js, vfs.js, bridge.js,
  fs.js, wasm_exec.js, then `s4qlInitSQLite()`, then the Go program with
  `-test.run=^TestEngine$`.
  - **Calls:**
    - `const engine = await Engine.start()`, then `await engine.run(request)`,
  then `await engine.state(t)`.
    - Calls run one at a time.
    - About 100 ms to ready, and about 100 ms per 2 fake minutes of 3 instances.
  - **Faults mid-run:** pause at T, add the action at T, run again. Events at
  exactly T can differ.
  - **Console noise:** the official build logs harmless OPFS errors.
  - **Seeds:** sent as JS numbers, so keep them below 2^53.

  ## Questions for you
  1. **File-system faults.** The agreed list includes them, but `core.Open` uses
  a VFS only through `Options.UseVFS`, which lives in `export_test.go`, and
  `Open` refuses `file:` paths. Reaching `faultvfs` would need an exported VFS
  option in `core.Options`, which is a design decision for you or Markus. A
  browser-only workaround of registering a fault VFS as SQLite's default would
  hit every instance at once.
  2. **The engine's own faults.** `loseAnswer`, `failRequest` and `damaged` are
  added in the engine's store client, as network faults the store contract
  allows (the core tests do the same with `damagingStore`). Please confirm these
  are acceptable alongside "whatever simstore offers".

⏺ The engine is built and in review as #32, with CI running. Summary so far:

  - It works in Chromium. The page sends a seed plus a schedule of faults, and
    gets the whole trace back. The same request always gives the same trace, and
    adding a fault at time T leaves everything before T unchanged.
  - Speed and size: ready about 100 ms after load, about 100 ms for two fake
    minutes with three instances, and roughly 3.5 MB to download compressed.
  - Faults it offers: S3 requests lost, slow, hung, refused or answered late;
    outages; broken reads; instances crashing, restarting and being cut off;
    wall and monotonic clocks shifted or stopped.
  - It also checks six invariants over each run, for example that every
    acknowledged commit is in the log exactly once. That's a checker the builder
    wrote for the page, separate from the one in core's tests.

  One thing for you to decide, not blocking: disk faults aren't reachable. core
  only lets its own tests swap in the fault-injecting file layer, so the page
  can't make an instance's disk fail without a new exported option in
  core.Options. You said no changes to core, so I've left disk faults out. Tell
  me if you want that option added.

› Message from fabrik:builder
  ## Summary

  The simulator page now runs the real storage code, the engine from #31 and
  #32, and the JavaScript model is deleted. The Pages workflow builds the engine
  into the site from the deployed commit. `make site-check` checks the page in
  headless Chromium, and CI runs it. `make lint`, `make test`, `make
  check-browser` and `make site-check` all pass, and the check passed 30 of 30
  runs with ten at a time. I did not run `make test-browser`, because no Go that
  runs in the browser changed. Nothing is pushed.

  **Branch:** `worktree-agent-accb65e123801d400`, 10 commits on `main`
  (`c2d8050` to `4f23bab`). Review with `git diff
  main...worktree-agent-accb65e123801d400`.

  ## What the page does

  Files: `site/simulator/index.html`, `site/simulator/run.js`,
  `site/simulator/page.js`.

  - **Loading:** the engine loads in its Worker. While it loads, the field in
  the bar says "Loading the engine.", and the status line gives the size. If
  WebAssembly or Workers are missing, or loading fails, a clear message replaces
  it. I checked the failure by blocking `engine.wasm`.
  - **Default run:** seed `0x7334716c`, the old page's seed. Three instances, 2
  min, the fault preset that the core's tests use (`simstore.AllFaults`), a
  crash of b at 0:40 and its restart at 1:00. It plays on load unless the
  visitor prefers reduced motion. Under this seed:
    - b takes the lease first;
    - c takes it over at about 1:14;
    - a takes it at about 1:49.
  - **Moving through fake time:**
    - Play and pause, at 1×, 4×, 16× or 64×.
    - Back and Step go to the previous or next event in the log.
    - A field of event bars in the sticky bar is also the scrubber: an invisible
  range input sits over it, so keyboard and touch work.
    - Each time in the log, the schedule, and the in-flight lists can be
  selected to go there.
  - **State at the current time:** the page asks the engine for it, so it has no
  second copy of the fold.
    - Instances: state, process, holder, whether it holds the lease and whether
  the manifest names it ("writer"), how far its cache is behind the head, both
  clocks with offset and stopped flags, cut off or not, calls and requests in
  flight.
    - The bucket: the manifest, and the commit objects as barcodes, marked head,
  in the log, or not in the log.
    - Requests and calls in flight, each with its phase.
    - The six invariants up to the current time, plus a summary at the end of
  the run.
  - **Event log:** every event kind in plain words. Routine events are hidden
  unless "Every event" is ticked.
  - **Adding faults:** each lands just after the current time. The page asks for
  the run again, and everything before that time stays identical.
    - Per instance: write, read, crash or restart, close or open, cut off, wall
  −60 s and +60 s, stop or start the wall clock, stop or start the monotonic
  clock.
    - Global: outage, calm, workload on or off, and lose the answer to (or fail)
  the next request of a chosen purpose, from any instance or one.
  - **Schedule:** lists each action with what became of it once its time is
  reached, with Remove and Remove all.
  - **Settings:** seed (plus New seed), number of instances (1 to 5), length (1,
  2, 5 or 10 min), lease, latency, buggify, workload (callers, pause, %
  writes), every random fault rate of S3 with the two presets, and Default run.
  - **What visitors are told:**
    - In plain words: this is `internal/core` unchanged, compiled to
  WebAssembly, on the official SQLite WebAssembly build, with a simulated S3 and
  simulated time.
    - "Built from" links the commit, read from `engine/build.json`.
    - The closing section ("What the page simulates, and what it leaves out")
  says what the page leaves out: disk faults, clock rates, process faults at
  random, and planted bugs.
    - No planted bugs appear anywhere.

  **Screenshots** (in `/private/tmp/claude-502/-Users-maragubot-Developer-s4ql/6
  8c0d47f-ada8-4603-995b-2482fe6fef3a/scratchpad/`):
  - Desktop at 1440 wide: `desktop-top.png`, `desktop-run.png`,
  `desktop-full.png`
  - Phone at 390 wide: `phone-run.png`, `phone-full.png`
  - Engine failed to load: `fail.png`

  The full-page phone capture is 390 px wide, so nothing scrolls sideways.

  ## URL format

  `#v=1&seed=<hex>&n=<1-5>&end=<ms>&lease=<ms>&latency=<ms>&faults=all|none|key:
  value,...&buggify=0|1&callers=&think=&writes=&s=<at>.<do>.<params>~...&t=<ms>`

  - Example action: `30001.crash.a~45001.cutOff.c.20000`.
  - `t` (the current time) is written when the run is paused or a link is
  copied.
  - A link without `v=1` (such as the model's old links), or with an unknown
  key, or with anything out of its limits, gives the default run and a note.
  - Pasting a new link into the address bar reloads the page with it. Links
  inside the page, such as the skip link and the checker chip, only scroll, and
  the run is kept.
  - Seeds go up to 2^53 − 1.

  ## Build and deploy

  - **`internal/simulator/serve`** has four new flags, with tests:
    - `-demo=false` leaves out the engine's demo page;
    - `-commit` writes `build.json`;
    - `-root` serves a directory other than the one built into;
    - `-isolated=false` drops the COOP and COEP headers.
  - **Make targets:**
    - `make site` builds into `site/simulator/engine/` (covered by
  `.gitignore`), with no demo page, so nothing shadows the page;
    - `make site-serve` serves `site/` on `SITEADDR` (default 127.0.0.1:8081)
  without COOP/COEP, as Pages does;
    - `make site-check` runs the smoke check.
  - **`pages.yml`:** sets up Go from `go.mod`, runs `make site
  SITECOMMIT=$GITHUB_SHA`, then uploads. It deploys on pushes to `main` that
  touch `site/**`, `internal/**`, `go.mod`, `go.sum`, the `Makefile` or the
  workflow itself, and on manual dispatch. Action versions keep their existing
  pinned style.

  ## Smoke check

  `internal/simulator/sitecheck` serves `site/` as Pages does and drives the
  page through an embedded harness in headless Chromium. Every wait is on a data
  attribute the page sets (`data-engine`, `data-runs`, `data-hash`), not on
  time. A 10-minute limit only stops a hung check.

  It checks that:
  - the engine becomes ready and the default run has events and commits;
  - a crash of a at 30001 ms leaves the 370 events before it identical and
  changes the events and hash after it;
  - the checker chip keeps the page and its run;
  - the page's link reproduces the run (hash `c3d93500811bf989`) and opens at 30
  s;
  - an old-format link gives the default run;
  - the link round trip, `insert`, and 25 links that must be refused, as unit
  checks of `run.js` in Chromium.

  It takes about 2 s after the build. I added it to CI's `browser` job (`make
  site-check`, `--no-sandbox`). `internal/simlint` exempts it, as it already
  exempts the Chromium runner in `internal/sqlite/browser`.

  ## `site/index.html`

  Unchanged. Its only mention is the link "Open the simulator", and nothing
  there describes a JavaScript model, so it is still true.

  ## Engine

  Unchanged apart from `serve`. `internal/simulator/README.md` now describes the
  site build and speaks of the model page in the past tense.

  ## What the engine offers that the page does not expose

  - **Per-instance lease durations:** the page uses one lease for all instances,
  to keep the settings simple.
  - **`readers` and the workload's `timeout`:** left at their defaults.
  - **The `long` / `longBy` faults:** off in the preset.
  - **`lines`:** the scheduler's text trace, which is large and not meant for
  visitors.
  - **Lengths above 10 min:** a 30-minute run is about 1.5 s and 6.6 MB per
  re-run.
  - **Fractional action times:** the page uses whole milliseconds.
  - **Random process faults drawn from the seed:** the README suggests the page
  could add them to the schedule. I left them out; that is your call.

  - In-page anchors reloaded the page and lost the run.
  - A failed state request was retried on every frame.
  - After a refused run, Remove could delete the wrong action; the page now goes
  back to the shown run.
  - Card toggles were decided from the previous run's state.
  - `toString` and `__proto__` were accepted as request purposes in a link, and
  odd fault values got through.
  - The callers select ignored a value from a link.
  - The checker and the schedule did work per event on every frame.

⏺ Answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · A run on the simulator page has a fixed length today (2 fake minutes). 
     What should happen when playback reaches the end? → Keep going 
     (Recommended)
     · For the simpler simulator page: besides the three instances, play/pause, 
     speed, and the manual fault buttons, which of today's panels should stay? →
     Event log, Bucket
     · With no seed shown on the simulator page, should a run still be shareable
     by link? → What is the seed then used for?

⏺ The seed decides every random choice in a run. That still matters with no
  random faults:

  - The workload: which instance writes or reads next, and how long it pauses.
  - S3 latency: how long each request takes.
  - Scheduling: which task runs first when several are ready.
  - Random values in the code: each instance's holder ID and the lease's random
    value.

  So the seed stays in the engine either way. What's left to choose is whether
  you ever see it:

  - Fixed and hidden. Every visit shows the same stream of reads and writes. A
    link then only needs the faults you injected and when.
  - Random per visit, hidden. Each visit shows a different stream. A link then
    has to carry the seed too, still without showing it on the page.
  - No link at all. Fixed seed, no Copy link button, nothing in the URL.

  I'd take the first: one fixed run, with a link that holds only your faults.
  Which one?

✻ Cooked for 51s · done Wednesday 9:04 PM

❯ I also liked the local disk faults. What other faults did you cut?            

⏺ Disk faults were never in the new page. The old JavaScript model had them, but
  the real core only lets its own tests swap in the fault-injecting file layer,
  so the engine can't reach it. Getting them back means one small change to
  internal/core: the test-only option that picks the file layer becomes a normal
  field of core.Options. core is an internal package, so the public API doesn't
  change, and neither does behaviour. With that, the page could fail one
  instance's disk on its own. Do you want that change?

  Cut in this simplification, still in the engine
  - Fail the next S3 request: S3 answers with an error.
  - Lose the answer to other requests: I kept it only for the next commit. The
    engine can also do it for a lease renewal, a takeover, a read, an upload or
    a download.
  - Clean close and reopen of an instance.
  - Manual write and read buttons, and switching the workload off and on.
  - Wall clock forward 60 s. I asked for "set back" and "stopped" only.
  - Random S3 faults, drawn by the engine at a rate you set:
    - requests or answers lost;
    - requests that hang or are slow;
    - conflicts;
    - broken reads;
    - random cut-offs and outages;
    - writes that land long after they were sent, including after the sender
      crashed.
  - Buggify: marked points in core that do legal but unusual things.

  In the old model, never possible with the real code
  - Disk faults (see above).
  - Stalling and resuming a process.
  - Clocks running at a different rate.

  Tell me which of these you want back and I'll pass it to the builder while
  it's still working.

  ## What the page shows and offers
  - **Intro:** two short paragraphs and the "Built from" line with the commit.
  It says the code is the real Go code, unchanged, compiled to WebAssembly, on
  the official SQLite WebAssembly build, with S3 and time simulated. It frames
  s4ql as built for one long-running instance per database: that instance serves
  a stream of reads and writes, and a second stands by to take over when it
  fails. No seed, instance or time is named.
  - **Bar (sticky):** Play/Pause, speed (1×, 4×, 16×, 64×), the fake clock, the
  engine's time for the last run, and "Start over", which is always there. Below
  it is the bar field from 0 to 10 min. It shows only the past, with marks for
  your faults.
  - **Three cells:** a, b and S3.
    - Each instance card shows its state ("serves", "stands by", "believes it
  holds the lease", "crashed"), lease, cache position with barcode, clocks, and
  call counts. The card of the instance with the lease is inverted.
    - The S3 card shows the head, the lease holder, the manifest writes, an
  outage, and a lost answer that is armed but not used yet.
  - **Below the cells:** "Things to try" (four items, checked against seeds 7,
  99, 4242 and 12345), then the log ("What happens") beside the commit objects,
  and one sentence on what is left out.
  - **Log tiers:** reads and commits are plain text, the story of the lease and
  faults is bold, your faults are inverted. If the engine's checker ever finds a
  violation, it shows up inverted in the log, since the checker panel is gone.
  - Nothing is written to the URL, and an old fragment is ignored without a
  note. Reduced motion: no autoplay, Play works.

  ## Faults kept, and why
  - Per instance: Crash/Restart, "Cut off from S3 for 40 s", and "Stop/Start its
  monotonic clock".
  - S3: "Make S3 unreachable for 40 s" and "Lose the answer to the next commit".
  - 40 s is longer than the 30 s lease, so the lease runs out during a cut-off
  or an outage.
  - **The wall clock faults are left out, which departs from your list.** Native
  runs of four seeds showed that setting the wall clock back, setting it ahead,
  or stopping it changes nothing a visitor can see, on the holder or on the
  standby. Its only visible effect, combined with a stopped monotonic clock, is
  a stale read that the checker excuses. That needs the checker and a paragraph.
  - A stopped monotonic clock does show something: the other instance takes
  over. After the clock starts again, the instance refuses calls by its wall
  clock and "learns that b holds the lease now".

  ## The refused calls of the instance without the lease
  - The workload runs on both processes, so the standby refuses every call. In
  the log, a refusal starts a "spell": one line per instance and reason, such as
  "b refuses calls: it does not hold the lease.", until that instance serves a
  call again.
  - The same rule keeps a renewal without an answer and a failed catch-up to one
  line while a fault lasts.
  - The card counts them: "0 writes and 0 reads served, 36 refused, the last
  because it does not hold the lease."

  ## Extending the run
  - When playback comes within max(60 s, 2 s × speed) of fake time of the end,
  the page asks for 2 min more. Playback keeps going while that run is computed.
  - A fault or "Start over" holds playback until its new run is shown.
  - It stops at 10 min. "Start over" is then highlighted and Play is off.
  - Measured run times, headless Chrome, two instances with a crash and a
  restart, mean of five:

  | Fake time | Run time |
  |---|---|
  | 2 min | 70 ms |
  | 4 min | 146 ms |
  | 6 min | 222 ms |
  | 8 min | 300 ms |
  | 10 min | 375 ms |
  | 12 min | 457 ms |
  | 15 min | 632 ms |

  So I kept the limit at 10 min: a fault added near the end holds playback for
  about 0.4 s.
  - **Limitation:** there is one engine, and it cannot answer a request for the
  state while a longer run is in flight. The instance and S3 cards hold for that
  one run (up to about 0.4 s at 8 to 10 min) while the clock, log and counts
  keep moving. A second Worker would fix this at the cost of a second engine's
  memory; I left it out. To make a held card less visible, the cards no longer
  show ticking clocks, only whether the monotonic clock runs.

  ## What I deleted
  - From `run.js`: link encode/decode, the fault presets and table, request
  purposes, settings lists, invariants, `instanceName`, and the old `describe`.
  - From `page.js`: the scrubber, Back/Step, the checker's chip and table,
  settings, the schedule list, Copy link, the "In flight" panel, the "Every
  event" toggle, the `hashchange` reload, and the logic that went back to the
  old run after a refusal.
  - From `index.html`: the matching markup and CSS.

  ## The smoke check
  `check.html` holds for any seed, so there is no hook for the seed. It checks:
  - `run.js`: seeds are whole numbers below 2^53, the request has two instances
  and no faults or buggify, `insert` keeps order, and a spell of refusals gives
  one line per reason.
  - The first run has events, commits, served reads and no violations.
  - A crash of a just after the current time leaves the events before it
  identical and changes the run after it, and the hash differs.
  - The run grows past 2 min with the same seed and faults and the same events
  before the old end, and playback continues.
  - It stops at 10 min with "Start over" offered, and every run on the way keeps
  the events of the one before.
  - "Start over" gives a new seed and hash and plays.
  - Nothing is written to the address, and an old link is ignored without a
  note.

  It waits only on `data-*` attributes and element states, never on time. One
  check takes about 15 s.

  ## Changes outside `site/simulator/`
  - `internal/simulator/sitecheck/check.html` (rewritten).
  - `internal/simulator/sitecheck/main.go`: package comment, plus the flag
  `--window-size=1600,1200`. In Chromium's default 800 by 600 window the page's
  animation frames did not run inside the check's frame, so the page never
  played and the check hung.
  - `internal/simulator/README.md`: the run times above.
  - `docs/diary/2026-11-18-simulator-page.md`: steps 4 and 5.
  - `site/index.html` is unchanged; its "Open the simulator" link is still true.
  The engine, core, sim and simstore are unchanged.

  ## Self-review
  The code-review skill (medium) found eight issues. Fixed:
  - After a refused run, the next frame asked for the longer run again, in a
  loop. A refusal now stops the page, with "Start over" as the way out.
  - Playback stopped silently at the end when a longer run was late. The field
  now turns dashed, and the lead grows with speed.
  - The state was fetched on every frame; now at most every 100 ms while
  playing.
  - After a failure there was no way out. "Start over" now recovers.
  - A refused "Start over" could still autoplay the old run.
  - `REASONS` was in two places.

  Not changed:
  - Each longer run repeats the log wording for every event, a few ms at most
  for the 4,600 events of a 10-min run.
  - The cards hold during a longer run (above).

  ⎿  Backgrounded agent (↓ to manage · ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ A builder is on the engine side of this now. A second one will change the page
  once the engine is merged.

  Why there was a time limit, and how it goes away. Every fault you added made
  the engine re-run from zero, which costs more the longer the run is. That
  design was there so a link could replay a run. You've dropped replay, so the
  engine will run live: it advances fake time as you watch, applies an action at
  the current moment, and never restarts. Then there is no limit.

  Two things I assumed, so stop me if they're wrong:
  - Disk actions mean the change to core. The test-only option that picks the
    file layer becomes a normal field of core.Options. Nothing else in core
    changes.
  - "Stop" means a clean shutdown, where the instance releases its lease, as
    opposed to a crash. It does not mean freezing the process, which the real
    code can't simulate.

  How I read the other actions:
  - Hang network: the instance's S3 requests get no answer.
  - Disconnect network: they fail at once.
  - Error disk: the instance's cache file operations fail with an I/O error.
  - Hang disk: a cache file operation doesn't return. This one may not be
    feasible. The browser has one thread, so a blocked file call could freeze
    the whole simulation. The builder will try, and report if it can't be done
    honestly.
  - Clock: the wall clock jumps forward or back, and "pause" stops both of the
    instance's clocks.

  For the page builder afterwards:
  - No auto-start.
  - A turbo button (1× to 5×, off at start).
  - Only a and b in the columns, with the S3 info below.
  - No "Things to try".
  - "Actions" that look clearly like buttons.
  - Much less text outside the intro.

✻ Sautéed for 2s · done Thursday 9:13 AM

› Message from fabrik:builder
  ## Report: the live session and the new actions in the simulator's engine

  The live session and every requested action are built, except the disk hang. I
  checked that the disk hang cannot work in the browser and explain why below.
  A session that receives the same actions at the same fake times as a batch run
  gives the same events, steps, summary and hash. This holds natively and in
  Chromium.

  **Branch:** `worktree-agent-a951352dd0db610bc`. 9 commits on `main`, not
  pushed. Review with `git diff main...worktree-agent-a951352dd0db610bc`.

  **Checks:** all pass on the final code: `make lint`, `make check-browser`,
  `make site-check`, the simulator package natively, under `-race -count=2`, and
  in Chromium. `make test` and the full `make test-browser` passed before the
  last two small fix commits. After those fixes I re-ran only the simulator
  packages, natively, under race, and in Chromium.

  ### The diff to `internal/core`
  There is none. `core.Options.VFS` was already a field on `main`, from commit
  `192cba6`. The engine passes it.

  ### How the session works, and how I checked that fake time stands still
  - The first task of the run is the controller. It sets up the instances the
  same way a batch run's `main` does. It then waits for each command on a
  channel made outside the `testing/synctest` bubble, and answers it.
  - A wait on such a channel is not durable. So `synctest.Wait` in the scheduler
  loop does not return, no task runs, and fake time does not move.
  - The scheduler sees the controller as the running task, as it sees a batch
  `main` that is doing an action.
  - The channels sit in a new package, `internal/simulator/handoff`, because the
  simulation's lint (`simlint`) refuses channel waits in the engine. I added
  `handoff` to the lint's exemptions, with a reason.
  - **Test:** 50 `now` commands with `runtime.Gosched` between them. `t` and
  `steps` stay the same and no event appears. Advances land exactly on their
  target time.

  ### The live API in brief
  These are new message kinds of `Engine.Handle`, plus matching calls in
  `simulator.js`, kinds in `worker.js`, and a live panel on the demo page
  (Start, Advance, Play, End, Act):
  - **`start`** takes the settings: the request's fields minus end, schedule and
  lines. Defaults are 2 instances and `workload.keep` 100.
  - **`advance`** takes `{"by": ms}` or `{"to": ms}`.
  - **`act`** takes an action of a schedule, without `at`.
  - **`now`** takes `{}` or `{"commits": n}`; with `commits` the state lists
  only the last n commit objects.
  - **`end`** runs the final checks and returns the summary and hash.

  Every answer carries the events since the previous answer, so the answers
  together are the whole trace in order. A refused command answers with only
  `error`, and the session goes on. The session keeps a running state, not its
  events.

  ### The equivalence result
  - **Tested** for 3 seeds with every action kind, every fault, buggify and
  `keep` 20.
  - **Advance with `to`:** the replay must use `to`, not `by`. The scheduler
  delays the start of the first task, so a session starts above time 0.
  - **The one unavoidable difference:** an advance that ends at a time with no
  action wakes the controller once more than a batch run would. That is one more
  scheduler step and one more draw from the seed, so from that point the run
  takes a different, equally valid course.
  - **Events at the exact time of an action** that happen after the controller
  wakes arrive in the next answer.
  - **Batch runs are unchanged:** I copied the old package into the worktree
  temporarily and ran 16 requests through both versions. Hashes, events, steps,
  summaries and states at five times were all identical. The only difference is
  that the echoed request now shows `workload.keep: 0`.

  ### Each action: what I built, and what the code does under it
  These come from runs with 2 instances and seed 1. The action hits the lease
  holder `a` from 30 s to 90 s.
  - **Crash:** `b` takes the lease at 64.1 s. A restart opens at once, as a
  spare.
  - **Stop (`close`):** the core logs `stop` with outcome `closed` and releases
  the lease at 30.1 s. `b` takes it at 31.1 s. So a page tells a stop from a
  crash by the release, and by the fast takeover.
  - **Hang network** (`hangNetwork`, built in the engine's client of S3):
  requests made during the hang are not sent, so they never reach S3, and the
  caller waits until its own timeout. A request already on its way still reaches
  S3, but its answer is lost.
    - In the run: renewals end with an unknown outcome, the holder's lease runs
  out on its own clock at 57.7 s, its write fails at 61.9 s, and `b` takes over
  at 64.1 s.
    - After the hang ends, a renewal started during the hang still times out at
  94.7 s. `a` learns it lost the lease at 99.9 s.
  - **Disconnect network** (`disconnectNetwork`): requests fail at once and
  nothing is sent. Requests on their way are cut: the caller gets the error at
  once, and S3 may still apply them. Calls waiting in a hang also fail.
    - In the run: renewals fail at once. The lease runs out at 57.7 s, `b` takes
  it at 64.2 s, and `a` learns of the loss at 94.8 s.
  - **Error disk** (`failDisk`, through one `faultvfs` VFS per instance; the
  VFSes are pooled because a crashed process keeps its files open): nothing
  shows until the next commit.
    - At 32.0 s the step that commits the write into the local cache, which
  comes after the manifest swap in S3, fails with an I/O error. The core stops
  with a local failure and releases the lease at 32.1 s, and `b` takes it at
  36.2 s.
    - Reopening fails every 2 s. I extended the engine's supervisor so that it
  retries after a failed open, as an application would.
    - When the disk works again, the database reopens at 90.3 s.
    - On the spare, a failing disk shows nothing at all.
  - **Pause clocks** (`pauseClocks`/`resumeClocks`): the process does nothing
  while paused, because all its sleeps and timeouts stop. `b` takes over at 64.1
  s.
    - After resume, `a` serves 3 stale reads, which the checker flags but
  excuses because both clocks stood still. It learns of the loss at 95.3 s.
  - **Clock jump forward** (`shiftWall`) on the holder: calls are refused with
  `lease_expired_wall` until the next renewal (18 refusals), then everything
  carries on and nobody takes over.
    - Backward on the holder, or forward on the spare: nothing a visitor can
  see. That matches what the earlier page builder found.

  ### The disk hang: not built
  - **The blocker in the browser:** the hung file operation would have to park
  its task in the scheduler from inside SQLite, which leaves a SQLite frame on
  the JavaScript stack.
  - While that frame is there, no file operation of Go's `os` package can
  finish, because `fs.js` only calls back once the JavaScript stack is empty.
  - Every commit creates and removes a spool file through `os`
  (`internal/core/spool.go`). So the first commit of any instance during the
  hang would block outside the scheduler, and the run fails.
  - The official SQLite build is also compiled without thread safety, so calling
  into SQLite while another call is suspended is not supported.
  - **Natively it is feasible:** a callback from the C VFS into Go that parks
  the calling task.
  - **What it would take in the browser:** spool files that do not go through
  `os`, which needs a `core` option, plus SQLite calls that can be suspended. Or
  a hang placed before a call enters SQLite, which is a different fault.

  ### Numbers (Chromium headless, Apple M4)
  - **One fake minute of a default session:**
    - Minutes 1 to 5: 115 ms (first minute, warm-up), then 68, 39, 43 and 44 ms.
    - Minutes 56 to 60: 51, 30, 42, 51 and 54 ms.
    - With every random fault on, it is about the same.
  - **Per fake minute, natively:** 611 steps and 442 events in minutes 10 to 20,
  against 626 steps and 458 events in minutes 50 to 60. Event bytes go from 120
  KB to 129 KB, because keys and numbers gain digits.
  - **To keep that cost flat**, I added `workload.keep`: each write trims the
  table to its newest rows. Without it, every read scans a table that grows
  forever.

  ### What still grows, and what the page should do
  - **Go memory:** about 12.5 MB per fake hour (8.0, 14.3, 20.1 and 26.9 MB at
  30, 60, 90 and 120 min). Most of it is the scheduler's text trace, which `sim`
  keeps for the hash. The rest is what `simstore` keeps.
  - **SQLite memory:** flat without crashes (8 MiB after an hour). About 0.47 MB
  per crashed process (8.4, 12.1, 25.3 and 36.5 MB after 0, 20, 40 and 60
  crashes). I could not reduce this without `core`: closing a crashed process's
  connections needs `CloseCache`, which today exists only in `core`'s tests, as
  a normal method.
  - **Crashes slow the scheduler:** each crashed process leaves its tasks parked
  in the scheduler. A fake minute after 60 crashes took 89 ms against about 45.
  - **The page should:**
    - call `now` with `commits` set;
    - keep only the events it shows;
    - start a new session or Worker after a few fake hours, or after many
  crashes. `simulator.js` already restarts the Worker by memory before a
  `start`.

  ### Changes outside `internal/simulator`
  - `.golangci.yml`: the lint rule that bans `faultvfs` outside tests now
  exempts `internal/simulator/*.go`.
  - `internal/simlint/simlint.go`: exempts the new `internal/simulator/handoff`
  package.

  ### Self-review
  I ran the code-review skill at medium, and a writer sub-agent who checked the
  README claims in runs. Fixed:
  - **Fixed: `trim` could delete the row its own write had just inserted.** The
  checker would then report a committed write missing from the log. The writer
  found this; there is now a test that fails without the fix.
  - **Fixed: one excused violation made the checker slow for the rest of the
  run.** After a `pauseClocks`, every later fake minute would cost more. Now
  only problems with the log itself switch it to the slow path.
  - **Fixed: a read that returned the same row twice passed the checker's fast
  path.**
  - **Fixed: a disconnect did not end calls already waiting in a hang.**
  - **Fixed: a crashed process's calls stayed in the instance's list of calls on
  the network.**
  - **Fixed:** refused commands now answer with only `error`; comments that said
  a session starts at time 0; the doc comment and limit of `keep`, now 10,000.
  - **Left:** `trim` reads the whole table in each write. That costs the same as
  a read and is bounded by `keep`.

  ### What the page builder needs to know
  `internal/simulator/README.md` is the specification: commands, settings,
  answers, the new actions and their exact semantics, the new events (`hung`,
  `disconnected`, `on`, `deletes`), the new state fields (`networkHung`,
  `networkDisconnected`, `diskFailing`, `commitCount`, `logLength`), what the
  code does under each action, and the measurements.
  - Use `advance` with `by` for playback.
  - The fault toggles belong to the instance, so they survive a crash and a
  restart.
  - The diary is `docs/diary/2026-11-19-simulator-live-engine.md`, steps 1 and
  2.

  ### Decisions for you and Markus
  - **Session defaults:** `workload.keep` 100 and 2 instances.
  - **The supervisor now retries a failed open every 2 s.** Today's batch runs
  never hit that path.
  - **Two follow-ups would remove most of the growth.** Both need changes to
  packages this brief kept off-limits:
    - an option for `sim` to hash its trace as it goes instead of keeping every
  line;
    - `CloseCache` in `core` as a normal method, so the engine can close a
  crashed process's connections.

⏺ The engine side is built and in review as #44, with CI running. I ran its

  What changed
  - No time limit. The engine now runs live: it advances fake time as you watch
    and applies an action at the current moment. A fake minute costs about 45 ms
    after an hour, the same as at the start.
  - No change to core was needed. The file-layer option is already a normal

  ## What the page shows and offers

  - **Intro:** the heading "The storage code, under faults", your two sentences
  on what this is, and "Built from <commit> of internal/core, run by
  internal/simulator."
  - **Bar:**
    - Play / Pause.
    - Turbo 5×, with `aria-pressed`, off at load.
    - The clock, e.g. `0:41.2`, or `1:02:03.4` after an hour, labelled "fake
  time".
    - A status line, used only while loading.
    - The bar field: the last 2 fake minutes, filled from the left at first,
  then with the present at the right edge, and small marks for your actions.
  - **Each instance card:**
    - Head: the letter and one word: serves (the head inverts), stands by,
  thinks it holds the lease, starting, stopping, stopped, core stopped, crashed,
  or cannot open its database. The process name (a2) shows after a restart.
    - Facts:
      - Lease: held / none / "it thinks so; S3 names b".
      - Cache: short name with a barcode, then "head" or "N behind".
      - Clocks: "true time", or e.g. "paused, wall −8 s, monotonic −8 s".
      - Served: e.g. "13 writes, 34 reads".
      - Refused: e.g. "46, last: no lease".
  - **Actions, as three rows of buttons:**
    - Crash ↔ Restart, and Stop ↔ Start. These swap their label and have no
  `aria-pressed`.
    - Hang network, Disconnect network, Error disk.
    - Clock +60 s, Clock −60 s, Pause clocks.
  - **How on and off look:**
    - Hang network, Disconnect network, Error disk, Pause clocks and Turbo are
  toggles with `aria-pressed`. On, the small square fills and the whole button
  inverts.
    - Hover doubles the frame instead of inverting, so it never looks like on.
    - Disabled buttons have a dashed frame. Focus shows a 2px outline.
    - Play is framed like the rest, so Turbo is the only inverted button in the
  bar when it is on.
  - **When each action is offered:**
    - Network and disk work even on a crashed instance, as the engine allows.
    - The clock buttons and Stop are off for a crashed process.
    - Stop is off while the database is not open, because `run.go` ignores
  `close` then.
    - Start is offered only after your own Stop. A process whose core stopped
  closes and reopens by itself, and should not show Start in between.
    - All actions wait while another action is on its way.
  - **Below the cards:**
    - An S3 row: head with the log length, lease holder and duration, manifest
  writes.
    - The log: the last 100 short lines, newest first.
    - Commit objects: the last 8, each with a barcode, short name, key, writer,
  time, and head / in the log / not in the log.
  - **Log rules:**
    - The standby's refusals take one line per spell and a count on the card.
    - A stale read after Pause clocks is one bold line: "b serves a stale read
  after its clocks stood still, which the spec allows."
    - A violation the design does not excuse is an inverted alarm line.
  - **Loading and failure:** "Loading the engine, about 3.5 MB." while it loads.
  If it fails, an inverted box with the cause ends in "Reload to try again" or
  "Reload to start over".

  **Deleted:** the batch requests, extending the run, the limit, "Start over",
  the speed select, the S3 card and its faults, "Things to try", the "left out"
  line, the section intros, and `CHUNK`/`LEAD`/`LIMIT`/`SPAN`/`request`/`insert`
  in `run.js`. `<head>` title, description and Open Graph now name the new
  actions. Fathom stays.

  ## How playback drives the session

  - Each animation frame adds its real time, capped at 100 ms, to a debt. So a
  hidden tab does not jump ahead.
  - `pump` runs one command at a time: a queued action, then a queued skip, then
  a playback step.
  - A playback step advances by the debt (at most 250 ms of real time; anything
  beyond is dropped), times 1 or 5. Then it asks `now` for 8 commits.
  - Commands never pile up.
  - An action while paused goes to the engine at once. It takes effect at the
  session's time, and the card and log show it before you press Play.

  ## Smoke check and its hook

  The hook is `window.s4qlPage.skip(ms)`. It queues advances in the same pump as
  playback, and a visitor can reach it only through the console.

  The check covers:
  - The engine becomes ready, the page starts paused near 0 ms, and fake time
  stays put for 30 animation frames without Play.
  - Play gives steps where fake equals real; Turbo gives steps of exactly 5×.
  The log and commit objects grow. Pause holds fake time for 30 frames.
  - Each action on a has its effect in the state and is undone when switched
  off: hang, disconnect, disk, the wall clock at +60 s and back to 0, pause and
  resume (5 s behind after skipping 5 s), crash then restart (process a2), stop
  then start.
  - After a crash of the instance that serves, the other takes over, 33 to 38 s
  of fake time later in my runs.
  - Skip to 11:00: the log and commits keep growing, there are no unexcused
  violations, the page keeps at most 100 log lines, and Play still works.
  - Nothing is written to the address, and the `run.js` unit checks pass.

  Every wait is on `data-*` attributes, state, animation frames, or skipping
  fake time until the code has made a change; none waits on a duration. One
  check takes about 5 s.

  ## Things I decided that you may want to change

  - **1× is real time.** Without turbo a takeover takes about 35 real seconds.
  - **The page stops after 24 h of fake time,** with "The engine has used up its
  memory. Reload to start over." This stands in for the Go heap, about 12.5 MB
  per fake hour, which the page cannot measure. It also stops if SQLite's memory
  passes 1 GiB. Remove the 24 h stop if it reads too much like a limit.
  - **The clock jump is 60 s,** twice the 30 s lease.

  ## Self-review

  The code-review skill at medium found 9 things. I fixed 7:
  - Failed opens of the same process were reported only once, ever.
  - A failed restart was logged as "Ignored".
  - Start was offered during the automatic close and reopen.
  - A bug in the page was reported as "The engine stopped".
  - Only SQLite's memory was guarded (now the 24 h stop as well).
  - The log and barcodes were redrawn on every step.
  - Three check waits depended on a change happening at the same fake time.

  I left 2:
  - Stop stays off while the database is not open, because `run.go` ignores
  `close` then.
  - `page.js` keeps its own holder-name map beside the one in `teller`.

  ## Outside `site/simulator/`

  Only `internal/simulator/sitecheck/check.html` and `main.go`,
  `internal/simulator/README.md` (two places), and the diary. No engine change.
  `site/index.html` is untouched: its only sentence is the link "Open the
  simulator".

  ## Worth a look by eye

  - The intro and the log lines.
  - "thinks it holds the lease" in a card head after Pause clocks.
  - The 2-minute window of the field.
  - Whether 1× feels slow.

         +s C under the C address sanitizer with `go test -asan`, and the
         + other fuzz tests without it. A test binary links C if it links
         + a package outside the standard library with cgo files, such as
         + this one; the `Makefile` finds these with `go list`, so a new 
         +package falls on the right side by itself. The sanitizer finds 
         +faults in C code only, and `make test` and CI's job `test` run 
         +the other packages. Each fuzz test runs for `FUZZTIME`, which d
         +efaults to 30s. Go supports `-asan` only on Linux, so on macOS 
         +the target runs itself in a `golang` Docker container.         
      83 
      84  SQLite's lookaside allocator reuses memory inside its own slabs
          , where the sanitizer cannot see it, so a statement used after
          it is finalized would go unnoticed. A binary built with `-asan`
           therefore turns lookaside off on every connection it opens. Ot
          her builds keep it on.
      85 
      86  `make fuzz` runs the fuzz tests without the sanitizer. On Linux
          , `FUZZFLAGS=-asan` adds it. Go's fuzzer measures coverage of G
          o code only, so for the C code the fuzzing is close to random m
          utation of the seeds.
      87 
      88 -CI runs `make test-asan FUZZTIME=30s`.                         
      88 +CI runs the same work in jobs that run at the same time: `asan`
         + runs `make asan-test`, the four jobs `asan-fuzz` each run a sh
         +ard of the fuzz tests that link C with `make fuzz FUZZGROUP=c F
         +UZZFLAGS=-asan FUZZSHARD=i/4 FUZZTIME=30s`, and `fuzz` runs the
         + others with `make fuzz FUZZGROUP=go FUZZTIME=30s`.            
      89 
      90  ## Updating SQLite
      91

⏺ A builder is on the fix: every file the simulator loads will carry the
  deploy's commit in its URL, so the HTML of one deploy can never run scripts or
  WebAssembly from another, whatever the caches hold. It also adds a check that
  fails if any request lacks the version.

  Until that's deployed, a hard reload gets you the working page.

  You could also change the cause at the source. In Cloudflare, under Caching,
  set "Browser Cache TTL" to "Respect Existing Headers". Scripts would then get
  GitHub's 10 minutes, not 4 hours. The versioned URLs make that optional.
