› Message from fabrik:builder
  Every file of the simulator now loads at the version of its deploy, and the
  new site check proves it: all twelve requests the page makes carry
  `?v=<commit>`. A fault at start-up now ends in the failure box with "Reload to
  try again". `make lint`, `make test`, `make check-browser` and `make
  site-check` all pass, and `git status` is clean after a local `make site`.
  Nothing is pushed.

  ## Branch and commits
  Branch `worktree-agent-a26fb11273294d996`, six commits on `main`:
  - d4b536f `serve`: new `-page` and `-version` flags.
  - 255c01c The JavaScript passes the version along, and start-up errors end in
  the failure box.
  - 8d78f62 `Makefile` (`SITEDIR`, `SITEVERSION`, a second site check) and
  `pages.yml`.
  - 96b94b8 `sitecheck`: the new checks.
  - 85d3d43 Fixes from self-review.
  - e1b683a Diary step 8 in `docs/diary/2026-11-18-simulator-page.md`.

  ## Mechanism
  Only the HTML is stamped. Every other file gets the version by passing along
  the query of its own URL.
  - `serve -page <index.html> -version <v>` rewrites the page in place. It
  appends `?v=<v>` to ` src="page.js"`, ` href="page.js"` and ` href="run.js"`
  (the script tag and the two `modulepreload` links).
    - It refuses a version outside `[0-9A-Za-z._-]`, and a page that is already
  stamped.
    - It stamps before it builds anything. With `-page` alone it only stamps.
  - `page.js` reads `version = new URL(import.meta.url).search` and adds it to
  what it loads.
  - `simulator.js` adds its own query to the Worker's URL.
  - `worker.js` adds `self.location.search` to everything it loads.
  - Without a stamp every query is empty. So the files in git, a local `make
  site` / `make site-serve`, the engine's demo page and `make simulator-demo`
  all run without a version. I confirmed the demo's three runs in Chromium.

  One change outside the files you listed: `internal/sqlite/browser/bridge.js`.
  `s4qlInitSQLite(options)` now passes its options on to `sqlite3InitModule`.
  Called without an argument it behaves as before; `make test-browser
  BROWSERPKGS=./internal/sqlite/faultvfs` passes. I also added a few sentences
  to the two READMEs.

  ## Every URL the page fetches, and how it gets its version
  | URL | How it gets the version |
  | --- | --- |
  | `page.js` (script tag and `modulepreload`) | The stamp in the HTML. |
  | `run.js` (`modulepreload`) | The stamp in the HTML. |
  | `run.js` (import) | Now a dynamic `import("./run.js" + version)` in
  `page.js`. |
  | `engine/build.json` | `fetch("engine/build.json" + version)` in `page.js`. I
  kept it so a local build still names its commit. |
  | `engine/simulator.js` | `import("./engine/simulator.js" + version)` in
  `page.js`. |
  | `engine/worker.js` | `new Worker(new URL('worker.js' + version, base))`,
  with `version` from `simulator.js`'s own URL. |
  | `sqlite3.js`, `vfs.js`, `bridge.js`, `fs.js`, `wasm_exec.js` |
  `importScripts(...map(f => f + version))` in the Worker. |
  | `engine.wasm` | `fetch('engine.wasm' + version)` in the Worker. |
  | `sqlite3.wasm` | `s4qlInitSQLite({ locateFile: (file, prefix) => prefix +
  file + version })`. |

  How `sqlite3.wasm` is fetched: `sqlite3.js` fetches it itself, from
  `Module.locateFile('sqlite3.wasm', scriptDirectory)`. In a Worker,
  `scriptDirectory` is the Worker script's directory without its query, so
  before this change the file came without the version. Its wrapper of
  `sqlite3InitModule` calls a `locateFile` passed in the module argument first,
  which is the hook I used. The build's OPFS proxy Worker only starts on a
  cross-origin-isolated page, which the site is not.

  ## What `pages.yml` does now
  It runs `make site SITECOMMIT="$SITECOMMIT" SITEVERSION="$SITECOMMIT"`. That
  builds the engine and `build.json` as before, and also stamps
  `site/simulator/index.html` on the workflow's checkout. The step name and the
  header comment now say this.

  ## The new check and its result
  `sitecheck` has a `-version` flag. Its server records every request outside
  `/__check/`. The check fails unless:
  - every request under `/simulator/` for `.js`, `.wasm` or `.json` has exactly
  the query `v=<version>`, or no query when run without a version;
  - each of the 12 files above was requested.

  `check.html` also:
  - waits for the page to name its commit, so `build.json` has been served
  before the check ends;
  - loads `run.js` at the version for its own unit checks;
  - loads two broken pages and expects the failure box with "Reload to try
  again": `/__check/broken/no-run/` serves the site without `run.js`, and
  `/__check/broken/no-play/` renames the Play button's ID, which reproduces
  Markus's case. No step depends on timing.

  `make site-check` now checks `site/` without a version, then copies it, stamps
  the copy with `serve -page` (version `SITEVERSION`, or `SITECOMMIT` if that
  is empty), and checks the copy. It takes about 13 s in total.

  Results:
  - **Red first:** the new check against the old JavaScript hung until its 60 s
  timeout, because a page that fails at start-up never leaves
  `data-engine="loading"`.
  - **Mutations:** I removed the version from each of the nine places in turn on
  a stamped copy. The check failed every time and named the files that lacked
  the version.
  - **Repeats:** `make site-check` passed 15 times in a row before the review
  fixes and 15 times after them. 12 checks run at once (6 unversioned, 6
  stamped) all passed.

  ## Self-review
  I ran the code-review skill at medium. It raised ten points; I fixed six:
  - `make site-check SITEVERSION=x` stamped `site/` and then failed when it
  stamped the copy a second time.
  - The copy rebuilt the whole engine just to stamp one file.
  - A bad version was only refused after the engine was built.
  - The regex also matched `data-src=`.
  - The mark was drawn only after `run.js` had loaded.
  - A missing space in `const $ = (id)`.

  Not changed, with reasons in the diary:
  - A local `make site SITEVERSION=x` rewrites the tracked `index.html` and
  cannot be run twice. That is by design for the workflow's checkout, and the
  Makefile says so. A second run also removes the built engine before `serve`
  refuses.
  - The step that waits for the commit hangs if `build.json` is missing, as
  every step of the check would. `make site` always writes it.
  - `halt` shows nothing if the HTML has no `#failure` element.
  - The reviewer flagged the edit to `site/simulator/page.js` under the rule
  about `site/`. Your brief asked for it.

  ## A visitor with HTML of the previous deploy cached
  - **Right after this change deploys:** HTML from before it (cached up to 10
  minutes) still asks for `page.js` without a version. That visitor can still
  hit the old bug until that HTML expires.
  - **After that:** every HTML asks for its own version, and a script cached for
  four hours under one version is only used by HTML of that version.
  - **One gap remains:** HTML of deploy 1, still cached up to 10 minutes after
  deploy 2, asks for `?v=1`. GitHub Pages ignores the query, so a cache that
  lacks that URL gets deploy 2's file under version 1's name. This needs a cache
  holding some of deploy 1's files and not others, and a reload fixes it. If it
  ever shows up, `page.js` could compare a version stamped into its content
  with the one in its URL and halt when they differ. I did not build that.

  ## To verify on the live site after the deploy
  - The page source has `page.js?v=<merge sha>` in the script tag and in both
  `modulepreload` links.
  - In the network panel every simulator file, including the Worker's
  `sqlite3.wasm` and `engine.wasm`, carries `?v=<merge sha>`.
  - "Built from" shows the merge commit.
  - Cloudflare's cache key includes the query string: `curl -sI
  'https://www.s4ql.com/simulator/page.js?v=probe1'` and then the same with
  `probe2` should each give `cf-cache-status: MISS` (or `DYNAMIC`), never `HIT`
  on the first request. If Cloudflare ignores the query, the edge would serve
  old content under new versions and this fix would not work there.
  - After a later deploy, a normal reload shows the new sha on every file.

  - `make lint`, `make test` and `make check-browser` pass.
  - `make test-browser` passes for every package (`internal/simulator` in 46.0
  s).
  - `make site-check` passed 15 times in a row (10 before the review fixes, 5
  after), each run with the plain and the stamped copy.
  - The client, live and checker tests pass under `-race -count=2`.
  - The impeccable detector found nothing.

  ## How the client works in the engine (`internal/simulator/client.go`)
  - **Settings.** `"client": {"instance": "a"}` in a request or a session; empty
  means no client. The action `{"do": "client", "instance": "b"}` points it at
  another instance. It is ignored in a run without a client.
  - **The loop.** The client is a task of the run, not of a process, so nothing
  that happens to an instance ends it. It repeats a pause (1 ms plus up to
  `workload.think`, drawn from the seed) and one call. It uses the workload's
  settings for writes, timeout and `keep`. `workload.on` does not switch it.
  - **How a call goes.** The client records `started`, then hands the call to a
  task of the target's process and waits for that task. `sim` lets a task of the
  run wait for a task of a process, and that wait ends when the process
  crashes. A task of the run cannot wait through a process's clock, so the
  core's waits have to run in a task of the process.
  - **The outcomes I added:**
    - `down`: the call fails at once because the target cannot take it. It has
  not started, has crashed, its open failed, it has no database (still
  starting), or its database is closed or stopped. `error` says which.
    - `lost`: the process crashed while the call was on it. The call ends at the
  crash, and a write's outcome is unknown.
    - A target that does not hold the lease gives `refused`, as before. Every
  other outcome is the same as for the callers.
  - **A switch** takes effect at once. A call already on its way ends on the old
  instance.
  - **No timeout of the client's own.** Under `pauseClocks` the client waits
  until the resume. A timeout of its own could let the process end the same call
  a second time; diary step 10 lists it as future work.
  - **The checker** counts `lost` as unknown (it may or may not be in the log)
  and `down` as not committed (it never reached the process). To show this
  matters, I counted `lost` as not committed instead: the 24 seeds then gave 5
  violations of `not_committed`. So lost writes do land in the log.
  - **Trace and state.** Calls show `caller: "client"` with the instance and
  process they went to. A new event `client`/`started` names the first target.
  `State.client.instance` is the target now.
  - **How a session starts.** The engine orders it itself, with no change
  outside `internal/simulator`. With `standby: true`, only `a` starts at first;
  `b` gets a `process`/`waiting` event and the state `waiting`. A task of the
  run waits until `a` serves (by `core.DB.Wait`, up to `a`'s lease duration),
  then starts `b`, which stands by, and only then lets the client make its first
  call.
    - An action on an instance that has not started is ignored, except
  `hangNetwork`, `disconnectNetwork`, `failDisk`, `loseAnswer`, `failRequest`
  and `client`.
    - The page starts its session with `{seed, workload: {callers: 0}, client:
  {instance: "a"}, standby: true}`.
  - **Tests (`client_test.go` and others):**
    - `a` takes the lease before `b` starts.
    - The client keeps calling through a crash and restart, a stop and start,
  and a failed disk of its target.
    - A switch moves its calls, and a call in flight at the switch ends on the
  old instance.
    - The instance it does not call gets no calls.
    - A write held by a hang at a crash ends `lost` at the crash.
    - 24 seeds with every fault, crashes, stops and switches give no violation
  that is not excused.
    - Two synthetic checker cases.
    - Live and batch runs give the same events, steps, summary and hash for 3
  seeds with the client, `standby` and switches.
    - The cost of a fake minute does not grow: 1186 steps, 583 events in minutes
  10 to 20 against 1208 and 598 in minutes 50 to 60. That variant uses `think`
  1000 and `keep` 20 so the means are steady with only one caller.

  ## What I fixed outside the client
  The live equivalence test exposed an existing fold bug. `catch_up.commits` is
  an `int64` in the engine but a `float64` after JSON, and the fold only read
  the first. So a page folding the events itself lost a partial catch-up.
  `attrInt` in `state.go` now reads both.

  ## The page (`site/simulator/`)
  - **The client strip** sits above the two columns, as a ruled sheet: "Client",
  a two-half switch `a | b`, then Writes, Reads, Failed and Last failure.
    - The switch is two buttons with `aria-pressed`; the one that is on is
  filled.
    - The Last failure cell turns white while the client's latest call failed.
  That makes the refusal by the standby plain.
  - **The wire.** A 3 px line runs from the strip to the letter of the target's
  column. Below 720 px the wire is hidden and the target's card head shows a
  CLIENT tag instead.
  - **Instance cards** are unchanged, except that `b` reads "not started" for
  its first moment and only its network and disk buttons work then.
  - **Intro:** "Here one instance serves the reads and writes of a client, and a
  second stands by to take over." The `<head>` and the lines that load
  `page.js` and `run.js` are untouched.
  - **Log lines.** A run of failures of one kind is one line when it begins; a
  served call or a switch ends the run.
    - "Client: a is down." and "Client: b refuses, no lease."
    - "Client: a crashed during the write of key N, outcome unknown."
    - "You point the client at b."
    - Served reads and writes show as before.
  - **Last failure words:** "a is down: crashed", "b refuses: no lease", "a
  crashed during a write, outcome unknown".
  - No em dashes and no "not X, but Y" in the new copy.

✻ Crunched for 5s · done Thursday 1:23 PM

❯ In practice, no-one will stay on the page that long. What happens if it keeps 
  running in a tab somewhere?                                                   
  Searched for 1 pattern, ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ It depends on whether the tab is visible.

  In a background tab, nothing happens. Playback is driven only by the browser's
  animation frames, and browsers stop those in a hidden tab. Fake time stands
  still, the engine does no work, and memory stays flat. When you come back it
  picks up where it was, without jumping ahead, because each frame counts for at
  most 100 ms.

  In a visible tab left playing, it stops itself after 24 fake hours. Memory
  grows by about 12.5 MB per fake hour, so by then the engine holds roughly 300
  MB. The page then pauses and shows "The engine has used up its memory. Reload
  to start over." At 1× that takes 24 real hours, and with turbo on a bit under
  5.

  It also stops if SQLite's own memory passes 1 GiB. That would take about 2,000
  crashes, at roughly 0.5 MB each.

  So a forgotten tab costs nothing while hidden, and a visible one ends in a
  clear message, not a crash. I'd leave the memory growth alone.

✻ Worked for 12s · done Thursday 1:25 PM

❯ The client says: a is down/crashed. But from the outside, it can't know that. 
  Write the client status from the client perspective: unknown if no answer,    
  any error reported by the instance, etc.                                      

⏺ fabrik:builder(Client wording from its view)
  ⎿  Backgrounded agent (↓ to manage · ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Agreed, and a builder is rewording the client now. The rule it's working to:

  - No answer. If the instance is crashed, stopped or not started, or dies
    mid-call, the client only sees that no answer came. It never says why. For a
    write it adds "outcome unknown", since from outside it can't tell whether
    the write happened.
  - An error from the instance. If the instance answers with an error (no lease,
    lease ran out by its clock, write not committed, local failure), the client
    reports that error as given.
  - Served, as today.

  This applies to the client strip and the client's log lines only. The instance
  cards keep saying what's true of the instances ("crashed", "stopped"). The
  smoke check will fail if the client's text ever contains "down", "crashed" or
  "stopped".

  I'll review, merge and deploy it when it's done.

  ## What the client says for each outcome

  | Engine outcome | Old "Last failure" | New "Last failure" | Old log line |
  New log line |
  |---|---|---|---|---|
  | `down`, any reason, read | a is down: crashed / not started / stopped / … |
  no answer from a | Client: a is down. | The client gets no answer from a. |
  | `down`, write | same | no answer from a, write outcome unknown | Client: a
  is down. | The client gets no answer from a to the write of key N: outcome
  unknown. |
  | `lost`, read | a crashed during a read | no answer from a | Client: a
  crashed during a read. | same as `down` |
  | `lost`, write | a crashed during a write, outcome unknown | no answer from
  a, write outcome unknown | Client: a crashed during the write of key N,
  outcome unknown. | same as `down` |
  | `refused` | b refuses: no lease (and the other reasons) | error from b: no
  lease / last commit unknown / cache behind / lease ran out by its monotonic
  clock / lease ran out by its wall clock; a reason the page does not know is
  shown with spaces for underscores | Client: b refuses, no lease. | The client
  gets an error from b: no lease. |
  | `unknown` | write on a, outcome unknown | error from a: write outcome
  unknown | Client: the write of key N on a ends without an answer. | The client
  gets an error from a: write of key N, outcome unknown. |
  | `not_committed` | write on a not committed | error from a: write not
  committed | Client: the write of key N on a is not committed. | The client
  gets an error from a: write of key N not committed. |
  | `abandoned` | write on a rolled back | error from a: write rolled back |
  Client: a write on a is rolled back before its commit. | The client gets an
  error from a: write rolled back. |
  | `busy` | cache of a busy | error from a: cache busy | Client: the cache of a
  is busy. | The client gets an error from a: cache busy. |
  | `timed_out` | call on a timed out | error from a: timed out | Client: calls
  on a time out. | The client gets an error from a: timed out. |
  | `stopped` (the instance answers that it has stopped) | a is down: core
  stopped | error from a: database closed / local failure / cache not in the log
  / log broken, else out of service. Taken from the first cause in the error. |
  Client: a is down. | The client gets an error from a: database closed (and so
  on). |
  | `failed` | call on a failed | error from a: no database open / local failure
  / call failed | Client: a call on a fails. | The client gets an error from a:
  … |
  | any other outcome | call on a failed | error from a: call failed | Client: a
  call on a fails. | The client gets an error from a: call failed. |
  | `served` / `committed` | unchanged | unchanged | unchanged | unchanged |

  As before, a run of the same kind of failure takes one line, and a served call
  or a switch of the client ends the run. All no-answer calls on one instance
  are one kind. The exception: `unknown` and `not_committed` take one line each,
  so the log names the key of each write.

  ## The "Failed" count

  I kept it as one count and kept the label "Last failure". The strip's CSS is
  in the `<head>`, and its grid holds three counts plus the last failure.
  Splitting Failed into errors and no answer would need a fifth cell, which
  means changing the `<head>`. The last failure cell already shows which of the
  two kinds the last failure was.

  ## Checked in the browser

  I drove each state with `playwright-cli` at 1280 and 390 wide, on a fresh page
  each time:

  | State | Last failure | Client line in the log |
  |---|---|---|
  | Served | "none" | none |
  | Target crashed | "no answer from a" | "The client gets no answer from a." |
  | Target stopped (the card still says "stopped") | "no answer from a, write
  outcome unknown" | "The client gets no answer from a to the write of key 3:
  outcome unknown." |
  | Pointed at the standby | "error from b: no lease" | "The client gets an
  error from b: no lease." |
  | Write in flight at a crash (network hung 9 s first) | "no answer from a,
  write outcome unknown" | "The client gets no answer from a to the write of key
  2: outcome unknown." |
  | Disk failing | "no answer from a", after an error | "The client gets an
  error from a: local failure.", then a no-answer line once the core had stopped
  |
  | Network hung, 45 s | "error from a: lease ran out by its monotonic clock" |
  "error from a: write of key 4 not committed", then the lease error; the card
  meanwhile says "thinks it holds the lease" |

  Each wording holds only what a client outside could know.

  ## The check

  - The old words in `check.html` are replaced with the new ones.
  - New unit checks:
    - No answer reads the same for all six reasons of `down` and for `lost`,
  once for a read and once for a write.
    - Every outcome has plain words with no "down", "crashed", "stopped",
  "undefined" or "null". This covers every reason in `Object.keys(R.REASONS)`, a
  reason the page does not know, the causes of a stop, and an outcome it does
  not know: 42 texts.
    - A stop's first cause is the one shown.
  - On the live page, MutationObservers collect the "Last failure" text and
  every client line in the log for the whole check, and the same no-banned-words
  assertion runs on them.
  - After the switch to b: if b refused calls, the log has "The client gets an
  error from b: …".

  ## Self-review

  I ran the code-review skill at medium. It found nine things; I fixed six:
  - **Stop cause:** matching `: closed` anywhere in the error was replaced by
  the first cause after "the instance has stopped: ".
  - **Unknown reasons:** a new `reasonWords` is used by the client strip, the
  card and the instance's log line, so a reason the page does not know reads the
  same in all three.
  - **Run grouping:** a run's kind now includes the outcome, not just the words.
  - **`errorWords`:** it returns one string instead of pairs.
  - **Collected texts:** the unit checks and the page check each have their own
  set instead of one at module level.
  - **Reasons in the check:** they come from `REASONS` instead of a hand-written
  list.

  Not changed:
  - **A lost write inside a run of no answers gets no line of its own.** Before,
  every `lost` call took a line. To the client it is the same failure, and
  splitting reads and writes into two kinds would flood the log as they
  alternate. The cell still shows "write outcome unknown". Say if you want lost
  writes to always take a line.
  - **"The observers only see what was drawn."** The check advances 1 s of fake
  time per command and the page draws after each, which is far below the 100
  lines it keeps. This depends on the seed, not on machine speed. The unit check
  covers the words of every outcome.

  The package comment in `internal/simulator/sitecheck/main.go` still says "the
  client keeps calling a while a is crashed". That describes what the check
  does, not page text, and Go code was out of scope, so I left it.

✻ Cooked for 12s · done Thursday 2:12 PM

❯ Why is there sometimes a and sometimes a2?                                    
⏺ a2 is the second process of instance a. When you crash a and restart it, the
  engine starts a new process on the same machine, with a new holder ID, and
  names it a2. A third would be a3. The page shows that name on the card after a
  restart.

  The engine needs the distinction internally, because the crashed process and
  the new one are different lease holders. A visitor doesn't: it's still
  instance a, restarted.

  I'd hide it and always show "a". Should I add that to the change the builder
  is already making?

❯ We should also have a "reset clocks" button                                   
  ⎿  Message queued for delivery to af281405b8414b52c at its next tool

⏺ Added to the same branch. "Reset clocks" will sit with the other clock buttons
  on each instance card. It makes the clocks run again if they're paused and
  brings the wall clock back to true time.

  One detail: a real monotonic clock can't jump forward, so after a pause it
  stays behind true time. That has no effect on the code, which only uses it to
  measure durations. I've asked the builder to make the card read as normal
  after a reset, so it doesn't suggest a fault that isn't there.

  S3", no a2, and Reset clocks.

✻ Brewed for 18s · done Thursday 2:17 PM

❯ Should we distinguish between the two types of clocks? So there's a "pause    
  monotonic clock", and the other one can jump back and forth and pause?        
  ⎿  Message queued for delivery to af281405b8414b52c at its next tool 
     round. Sending this moved nothing it is running to the background just
     now, so the message waits for that round unless something moves 
     sooner. If it finishes first, it is resumed with the message and 
     notifies again, so wait for that notification before resending.

⏺ Yes, I think we should, and I've passed it to the builder. The two clocks are
  the point of that part of the design: before a read, the holder checks its
  lease by both, and both must agree. With one "Pause clocks" button, a visitor
  only ever sees the worst case.

  The clock actions per instance become
  - Wall clock: +60 s, −60 s, pause.
  - Monotonic clock: pause. It can't jump.
  - Reset clocks: both run again, and the wall clock returns to true time.

  What a visitor can then find out, going by the earlier measurements:
  - Monotonic paused alone: the instance stalls and the other takes over. After
    the clock starts again, the instance refuses calls by its wall clock. No
    stale read.
  - Wall clock wrong alone: at most some refused calls until the next renewal.
  - Both wrong: stale reads, which is the one case the spec accepts.

  I asked the builder to run all three on the lease holder and report what the
  page shows, so we know each tells its story. One caveat: pausing only the wall
  clock may show nothing a visitor can see. If so, I'll tell you.

  The cost is one more button: five clock controls where there were four.

  - **How reset works:** `startWall` and `startMonotonic` for any clock that is
  stopped, then `shiftWall` by the wall offset read from the state after those
  steps. Queued jobs can now hold a step that is worked out when its turn comes.
  - **Precision:** it lands exactly; the check reports "off by 0 ms". The engine
  keeps clocks in whole nanoseconds and rounds the shift to them.
  - **When Reset is on:** it is off while the clocks are untouched or the
  process is not running. After a reset neither Pause is pressed.
  - **Check:** every action has its effect and is undone by its opposite or by
  Reset, including a reset where only the monotonic clock was paused.

⏺ Agent "Client status cell" is waiting on its own background work · 21m 36s

  Created PR #58, ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ All five changes are built and in review as #58, with CI running. The smoke
  check passes for me.

  What changed
  - Status: the cell reads "no calls yet", then "ok from a" or the failure's
    words. It's highlighted only while the latest call failed.
  - S3: the buttons are "Hang S3" and "Disconnect S3", and the log says "You
    hang the requests of a to S3."
  - No a2: the page always names the instance. After a restart the log says "a
    starts again, on the cache that it left when it crashed." While the crashed
    process's lease is still in S3, the page says "a before its restart".
  - Clocks: a "Wall clock" row with +60 s, −60 s and Pause, a "Monotonic clock"
    row with Pause, and "Reset clocks". The card's clock value shows only what
    differs from normal, such as "wall +60 s" or "monotonic paused", and "true
    time" otherwise.

  One finding you should know. Pausing a clock on the instance the client is
  calling shows nothing. The client's writes keep committing, each commit renews
  the lease, and the instance keeps serving, with the status staying "ok from
  a". Only the card's clock value changes.

  The clock stories only appear when the client is pointed at the other
  instance, so the holder is idle:
  - Monotonic paused 60 s: the other instance takes over after about 28 s. When
    the client comes back, it gets "error from a: lease ran out by its wall
    clock". No stale read.
  - Wall clock paused 60 s: still nothing visible, and a keeps serving.
  - Both paused 60 s: the other instance takes over. In that run a's next call
    was a write, which S3 refused, so no stale read showed. A read first would
    have been stale.

  So the clock buttons are mostly quiet unless a visitor also switches the
  client away. That is how the code really behaves, but it makes the clock
  controls the least rewarding part of the page. Options, once you've tried it:
  keep them as they are, drop the wall clock pause since it never shows
  anything, or leave clocks out altogether.

  **1. Status**
  - **Values:** "no calls yet" before the first call ends, then "ok from a" or
  "ok from b" after a served or committed call.

  clear which instance answered.
  - **Check:** at zero it reads "no calls yet". While a serves it reads "ok from
  a". While a is crashed it shows the no-answer words with the highlight on.
  After the switch to b it names b, and is highlighted only for a failure.
  - **After the restart:** the check cannot just wait for "ok from a". a2 and b
  both wait for a1's lease to run out and either can take it; both happened in
  my runs. So it waits for an instance to serve, points the client there, waits
  for "ok from" that instance with the highlight off, then points back at a.

  **2. S3**
  - **Buttons:** "Hang network" and "Disconnect network" are now "Hang S3" and
  "Disconnect S3".
  - **Hang S3 log lines:** "You hang the network of a." becomes "You hang the
  requests of a to S3.", and "The network of a no longer hangs." becomes "The
  requests of a to S3 no longer hang."
  - **Disconnect S3 log lines:** "You disconnect a." becomes "You disconnect a
  from S3.", and "You connect a again." becomes "You connect a to S3 again."
  - **`<head>`:** "hang or disconnect its network" becomes "hang or cut its
  connection to S3", and "cut its network" becomes "cut it off from S3".

  **3. Instances instead of processes**
  - **Card:** the process span on the card head is gone, with its CSS.
  - **Rest of the page:** the commit writer, the S3 lease holder and the log use
  `instanceOf`.
  - **Lines with two processes of one instance:**
    - "a2 starts on the cache that the crashed process left." becomes "a starts
  again, on the cache that it left when it crashed."
    - "a2 takes the lease over from a." becomes "a takes the lease again."
    - "a2 learns that a holds the lease." becomes "a learns that its lease from
  before has not run out yet."
    - When the manifest names the process itself, it reads "a learns that it
  lost the lease."
  - **Lease of a crashed process still in S3:** the S3 row and the card now say
  "a before its restart". Before this, the S3 row read "a, 30 s" while a's card
  said it stands by.

  `/\b[a-e]([2-9]|[1-9][0-9]{1,2})\b/`, checked after a's restart, after the
  holder's restart, and at 11 min. This is wider than your pattern so that `a10`
  and up are caught. It stops at three digits because unlimited digits matched
  a commit's short name, "b8828120".

  **4 and 5. Clocks**
  - **Rows on each card:** "Wall clock" has +60 s, −60 s and Pause (a toggle).
  "Monotonic clock" has Pause (a toggle). Reset clocks stands apart at the right
  of the second row. At 390 the wall row is three columns and Reset clocks has
  a row of its own. Nothing crowds at either width.
  - **Card value:** each clock only where it differs, for example "wall +60 s",
  "wall paused, −5 s" or "wall −5 s; monotonic paused", otherwise "true time". I
  dropped the monotonic offset; the card says only whether that clock runs, so
  after a reset it reads "true time".
  - **Log:** "You pause the wall clock of a.", "You start the wall clock of a
  again.", the same pair for the monotonic clock, and the existing "You move the
  wall clock of a forward 60 s." One reset gives one line, "You reset the
  clocks of a.", and one mark on the field. A reset step that fails reads "You
  reset the clocks of a. Ignored: …".
  - **Stale read line:** "a serves a stale read after both of its clocks went
  wrong, which the spec allows."
  - **How reset works:** `startWall` and `startMonotonic` for any clock that is
  stopped, then `shiftWall` by the wall offset read from the state after those
  steps. Queued jobs can now hold a step that is worked out when its turn comes.
  - **Precision:** it lands exactly; the check reports "off by 0 ms". The engine
  keeps clocks in whole nanoseconds and rounds the shift to them.
  - **When Reset is on:** it is off while the clocks are untouched or the
  process is not running. After a reset neither Pause is pressed.
  - **Check:** every action has its effect and is undone by its opposite or by
  Reset, including a reset where only the monotonic clock was paused.

  **Clock cases on the holder (a, 60 s each)**
  - **Client calling the holder:** in all three cases nothing shows except the
  card's clock value and the visitor's two lines. The card says "serves"
  throughout and the status stays "ok from a". The client's writes commit, and
  each commit renews the lease. A visitor who pauses a clock of the serving
  instance therefore sees no story while the client calls it. That is a question
  for you and Markus.
  - **With the client pointed at b during the pause and back at a after:**
    1. **Monotonic paused 0:20.0 to 1:20.0:**

    2. **Wall paused 0:20.0 to 1:20.0:** nothing shows except the card ("wall

    3. **Both paused 0:20.0 to 1:20.0:**

✻ Brewed for 1m 10s · done Thursday 2:46 PM

❯ Oh wait, I forgot the client isn't external, but in the app. So it would also 
  crash when the app crashes.                                                   

⏺ You're right about s4ql: it's embedded, so the code that calls it lives in the
  app's process and dies with it.

  The simulation already works that way underneath. Each call from the "client"
  is handed to the target instance's process and runs inside it. If the process
  crashes mid-call, the call dies with it, and the outside only sees that no
  answer came.

  So what the page calls "Client" is really the app's users: something outside
  the app that sends it requests, such as HTTP traffic through a load balancer.
  Each instance on the page is one running copy of the app with s4ql inside.
  Read that way, everything on the page holds:

  - "No answer from a" is what a user sees when the app process is gone.
  - "Error from b: no lease" is the app passing s4ql's error back.
  - The a | b switch is where the traffic is routed.

  That makes it a naming fix, with no change to the engine. I'd rename "Client"
  to "Users" throughout, including the log ("Users get no answer from a") and
  the intro. The intro would say that each instance is a copy of the app with
  s4ql embedded, and that its users' reads and writes go to one of them.

  The alternative is to go back to callers inside each process. Then the stream
  stops whenever that instance is down, which is what you wanted to get away
  from.

  Rename to "Users", or do you prefer another word, such as "Traffic" or
  "Requests"?

✻ Cooked for 16s · done Thursday 2:59 PM

❯ I'm reversing my decision from earlier. There is no load balancer, maybe an   
  HTTP proxy. The typical use case is one app instance serving requests         
  (responding over HTTP to browsers basically) on one machine, just like when   
  you would use SQLite in a server-side context.

  only the words change.

  - The status keeps its words: "ok from a", "no answer from a", "error from b:
    no lease".
  - The log speaks of requests, for example "A request gets no answer from a."
    and "You send the requests to b."
  - The intro will say that an instance is the app with s4ql embedded, that

    load balancer.
  - "Client" disappears from every text on the page, and the smoke check will
    fail if it comes back.

  This matches how the engine already works: a request is handled inside the
  instance's process and dies with it, and from outside it just gets no answer.

  I'll review, merge and deploy it when it's done.

⏺ First open item: the clock controls on the simulator.

  While requests go to an instance, pausing its clocks shows nothing except the
  clock value on its card. Its commits keep renewing the lease, so it keeps
  serving. The takeover only appears if you first send the requests to the other
  instance.

  I'd keep the monotonic clock's Pause and the wall clock's +60 s and −60 s, and
  drop the wall clock's Pause, because it showed nothing in any case we ran.
  Keep, trim that one, or drop the clock controls altogether?
✻ Worked for 6s · done Friday 10:21 AM

❯ Why doesn't the other instance take over when clocks are paused and thus the  
  lease doesn't get renewed?                                                    

  Searched for 1 pattern, ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The lease does get renewed, by the commits.

  The holder renews in two ways. A timer sends a renewal when nothing else has
  happened for a while, and that timer does stop with the paused clock. But
  every commit also swaps the manifest, and that swap carries a fresh lease, so
  each write is a renewal too.

  The standby takes over only after it has watched the manifest stay unchanged
  for longer than the lease, by its own clock. While requests keep writing to
  the paused instance, the manifest changes every few seconds, so the standby
  never sees it stand still.

  The takeover appears once the writes stop reaching that instance: then only
  the stopped timer could renew the lease, and it can't. That's why it shows up
  when you send the requests to the other instance first.

  Keep, trim the wall clock's Pause, or drop the clock controls?

✻ Sautéed for 13s · done Friday 10:23 AM

※ recap: We're refining the live s4ql simulator page, which now runs the real storage code with a Requests strip and per-instance actions. Next, you decide whether to keep the clock controls, drop only the wall clock's Pause, or remove them all.

❯ Ah, right. But the lease gets a wrong clock, no? So why doesn't b take over?  

  Searched for 1 pattern (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Yes, the lease carries the holder's wall-clock time, and with a paused clock
  that time is wrong. But b never reads it. In the code, that timestamp is
  written into the manifest and nothing in the storage core reads it back to
  make a decision.

  b decides by one thing only: whether the manifest has changed. Each renewal
  and each commit writes a new random value into it. b takes over when it has
  seen the manifest unchanged for longer than the lease, measured on its own
  monotonic clock.

  That's deliberate. The spec says no instance compares its clock with
  another's, because two machines' clocks can't be trusted to agree. So a holder
  with a wrong clock can't trick b into taking over early, and it also can't
  trigger a takeover by writing a stale time.

  Keep, trim the wall clock's Pause, or drop the clock controls?

                Requests  [a | b]  status
                   │
          ┌────────┴─────────┐
     ┌────┴─────┐       ┌────┴─────┐
     │ a serves │       │ b stands │   Crash, Stop sit on the instance
     │          │       │    by    │

       │  │   └ clocks    │  │   └ clocks    +5 s, −5 s, Pause, Reset

       │                  │
       └────────┬─────────┘                  Hang, Disconnect sit on each edge
             ┌──┴──┐
             │ S3  │  head, lease, manifest writes
             └─────┘

  ## The row
  - Label "Clocks", then:
    - "Wall +5 s" sends `shiftWall` 5000.
    - "Wall −5 s" sends `shiftWall` −5000.
    - "Pause" has the LED and `aria-pressed`. It sends `pauseClocks`, or
  `resumeClocks` when the clocks stand still.
    - "Reset" sends `resumeClocks` if the clocks are paused, then the
  `shiftWall` back to true time, as before.
  - Screen readers hear "Move the wall clock of a forward 5 s" (and back),
  "Pause both clocks of a", and "Reset the clocks of a".
  - At 1280 it is one row. At 390 it is not: about 324 px inside the card is too
  narrow for four buttons, so the label takes its own line and the buttons fall
  into a 2 by 2 grid, as every other action row does at that width. There is no
  horizontal scroll at 390.

  paused".
  - New: "normal", "wall +5 s", "paused", "paused, wall −5 s". After a reset it
  says "normal".
  - I read "normal when untouched" in your brief as the word itself. It is also
  truer than "true time" after a pause, because the monotonic clock stays
  behind. It is a one-word change if you prefer the old wording.

  ## Log lines
  | Action | Old | New |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | Jump | "You move the wall clock of a forward 60 s." (and back) | "You move
  the wall clock of a forward 5 s." (and back) |
  | Pause | "You pause the wall clock of a." / "You pause the monotonic clock of
  a." | "You pause the clocks of a." |
  | Pause again | "You start the wall clock of a again." / "You start the
  monotonic clock of a again." | "You start the clocks of a again." |
  | Reset | "You reset the clocks of a." | unchanged |
  | Stale read | "a serves a stale read after both of its clocks went wrong,
  which the spec allows." | "a serves a stale read after its clocks were paused,
  which the spec allows." |

  The stale-read wording holds because the checker excuses a stale read only if
  the monotonic clock was stopped, and on the page only Pause stops it, together
  with the wall clock.

  The lines for `stopWall`, `startWall`, `stopMonotonic` and `startMonotonic`
  are gone from `run.js`, because the page no longer sends those actions.

  - What it asserts on a:

  lease and restarts it, but leaves the requests where they were. If that
  instance was a, the restarted a only stands by and refuses every request. In

❯ What does "Field seed" in the  hero mean?                                     

⏺ It's the number that generates the pattern of bars behind the headline. The
  bars come from a seeded random generator, and 0x7334716c is the seed: the four
  letters "s4ql" as bytes. The same seed always draws the same pattern.

  I put it there as a nod to the project's testing, where a seed replays a
  simulation run exactly. It says nothing about the product, and it sits in a
  list of product facts, so it reads as one.

  Should I remove that row?

✻ Cooked for 34s · done Friday 11:26 AM

❯ WHen I click pause on the clocks in the simulator, it only says "wall -x s"   
  in the text. But it's both, right?                                            

⏺ Yes, Pause stops both clocks. The text is "paused, wall −x s": "paused" means
  both, and "wall −x s" is how far the wall clock has fallen behind real time
  since.

  Only the wall clock gets a number because only it claims to be the time of
  day. The monotonic clock just measures durations, so "behind" has no meaning
  for it, and the code never notices. That's also why the text still says "wall
  −8 s" after you un-pause: the wall clock stays 8 s behind until you press
  Reset.

  It's easy to misread as "only the wall clock is paused", though. I'd change it
  to "both paused" while paused, and show "wall −8 s" only after you start them
  again. Should I make that change once the diagram is live?

✻ Worked for 19s · done Friday 12:19 PM

› Message from Explore
  **1. SQL supported today** (README.md:17-31; docs/roadmap.md: slices 1-7 are
  "built" at :40, :66, :92, :123, :160, :203, :243, and slice 8 is "agreed, not
  built" at :294)
  - Statements: `create table` / `drop table` (strict, without rowid, exactly
  one primary-key column), single-row `insert` (with or without a column list),
  and `select` of named columns or `*` with a `where` of comparisons and `is
  [not] null` joined by `and`. Also `?` arguments, prepared statements, and
  transactions, including read-only ones.
  - Types: `int`, `integer`, `real`, `text`, `blob`, all exact-typed, with
  nullable columns.
  - Not built: `update`, `delete`, `or`, `order by`, `limit`, multi-row
  `insert`, indexes, defaults, and a statement while rows of the same
  transaction are open.

  **2. Where it lives and how it opens**
  - Parser and statement pass: `internal/sql/{token,parser,ast,stmt,sqlerr}`.
  Executor: `internal/engine`.
  - Engine entry: `engine.New(d *core.DB, h host.Host, options Options) *Engine`
  (internal/engine/engine.go:90), then `Engine.Conn()` (:147). A `*Conn` has
  `Exec(ctx, src, args ...*s4qlpb.Value)`, `Query`, `Begin`, `BeginReadOnly`,
  `Commit`, `Rollback` and `Close` (internal/engine/README.md:7-21).
  - The engine sits on an already-open `*core.DB` plus a host (the clock). It
  takes no store or config of its own.
  - There is a `database/sql` driver in the root package `maragu.dev/s4ql`:
  `Open(ctx, Options) (*sql.DB, error)` (s4ql.go:304) and `Register` (:335). It
  is hardwired to S3 and `host.New()` (s4ql.go:~345-359). Internally it calls
  `newConnector(ctx, h, s store.Store, o, co core.Options, ...)` (conn.go:72),
  which calls `core.Open(ctx, h, s, path, prefix, co)` and then `engine.New`
  (conn.go:93-104). That seam is unexported.

  **3. The simulator's workload today**
  - One table `t` with one INT primary-key column `k`
  (internal/simulator/run.go:65-74).
  - A write (run.go:593-624) runs `d.Begin`, then `tx.Table` / `tx.CreateTable`,
  then `tx.Upsert` of the row `[k]`. An optional trim (`tx.Scan` + `tx.Delete`,
  run.go:631-652) keeps `Workload.Keep` rows. Then `tx.Commit`, where
  `core.ErrOutcomeUnknown` maps to the outcome "unknown".
  - A read (run.go:657-679) runs `d.Reader`, `rd.Read`, `rtx.Scan(t)`, and
  returns the keys plus `rtx.Position()`.
  - The keys are unique values from a counter that starts at 1
  (internal/simulator/README.md:131).
  - All invariants identify a transaction by its key (README.md:614-625,
  check.go:19-40). The checker gets the keys and deletes of each commit from the
  `UpsertRows`/`DeleteRows` changes of the commit object (run.go:1378-1390).
    - `acknowledged`, `not_committed` and `once` need one key per write in the
  log.
    - `state` needs a read's keys to equal the rows of the log at its position.
    - `fresh` needs a read's keys to include every write acknowledged earlier,
  minus keys that were deleted.
  - An SQL `insert` keeps this shape. But the trim (`keep`, which a live session
  sets to 100 by default) needs `delete`, which is slice 8 and not built.

  **4. Building for the browser**
  - `GOOS=js GOARCH=wasm go build -tags s4ql_browser` succeeded for
  `./internal/engine`, `./internal/sql/{stmt,parser,ast,token,sqlerr}` and even
  the root package `.`.
  - The engine imports only core, format, host, s4qlpb, sqlerr and stmt: no cgo
  and no `internal/sqlite` directly. Its waits go through `host`
  (internal/engine/README.md:289 says no `iter.Pull`).
  - `go vet` of the tests fails under wasm. The cause is `internal/sql/oracle`
  (`DenyProcessPragmas` is undefined, oracle.go:268), which the engine's
  `differential_test.go` imports, plus `token_test` (`sqlite.Tokenize`).
  - No SQL package is in `BROWSERPKGS` (Makefile:224-225), in `SIMULATORPKGS`
  (:228), or in CI's browser simlint list (ci.yml:338).

  **5. Storage:** the engine calls `core.Tx.Insert`, `Upsert`, `CreateTable` and
  `Annotate` (internal/engine/storage.go:246-268). So rows land in real SQLite
  tables in the core's cache (internal/core/README.md:44, :62). At commit, the
  core writes the changed rows into the commit object as `UpsertRows`, together
  with an annotation of the SQL text and its arguments.

  **6. Existing SQL-level simulation:** `internal/engine/simulation_test.go` has
  `TestSimulation` (:1966) and `TestSimulation_plantedBugs` (:2123). They run
  on `internal/sim` and `simstore` with crashes, store faults and transactions,
  and compare results with SQLite (internal/engine/README.md:515-517). It is
  test-only code, and its SQLite comparison likely uses the cgo oracle. I found
  nothing in docs/todo.md or docs/spec.md about running SQL through the browser
  simulator. The only simulator mention is at todo.md:206 (the simulator opens
  with an empty identity).

⏺ Yes, and it is cheap: the SQL engine already builds for the browser unchanged.
  It sits on an open core.DB and a clock, which the simulator already has, and
  it needs no SQLite of its own.

  What I would do:

  - Requests become SQL. A write is insert into t (k) values (?) and a read is
    select k from t, both through internal/engine, the same code the
    database/sql driver runs on.
  - The page shows it. The log and the Requests strip show the statement. The
    commit objects already carry the SQL text and its arguments as an
    annotation, so the barcode detail can show them too.
  - The checker stays. It identifies each write by its key and reads the keys
    from the commit objects, which an SQL insert writes in the same shape.

  One catch: the simulator trims the table to 100 rows so that memory stays
  flat, and that needs delete, which is slice 8 and not built. Until it is, the
  trim would stay a direct call on the core, and the page would show only the
  inserts and selects as SQL. When slice 8 is merged, the trim becomes a delete
  statement.

  The simulator would use internal/engine and not the public s4ql.Open, because
  that one is hardwired to real S3 and the real clock. That keeps the rule of no
  changes to the Go code outside the simulator.

  Shall I start a builder on this now, with the trim on the core until slice 8?

⏺ Agent "SQL layer vs simulator facts" finished · 1m 39s

❯ Could you just write to local storage in the browser instead? To act as s3.   
  No need for trimming then. And we'll add delete once that's done.             

  Searched for 1 pattern (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ That would not remove the trim, because the trim is not about S3. A read scans
  the whole table, so without a bound each read gets slower and its list of
  keys in the log longer as the session ages. Where the commit objects are
  stored does not change that. Local storage is also capped at about 5 MB per
  site and survives a reload, against "refreshing the page means starting over".

  What does remove it is a bounded read, which the SQL we have can do:

  - Write: insert into t (k) values (?), with no trim.
  - Read: select k from t where k > ?, with the bound 100 below the newest key.

  The table then grows for the whole session, at a few bytes per row, and the
  page's stop at 24 fake hours covers that. The checker changes to compare a
  read with the rows above its bound, which is simulator code only.

  One thing I do not know yet: whether the engine turns where k > ? on the
  primary key into a range scan, or scans the whole table and filters. If it
  filters, a read still slows down with age. The builder measures that first; if
  it is too slow, the fallback is the trim on the core until delete exists.

  When slice 8 is merged, delete comes in as a visible request and not as a
  hidden trim.

  Shall I start the builder on that?

    that come back (restart, recovery, takeover), falling for things that go
    away (crash, stop, requests failing), one blip for a fault you switch, and a
    short alarm if the checker finds a violation.
  - Not on every request or commit, only on those events, so it does not tick
    constantly.
  - Volume: peak at about -24 dBFS, the alarm at about -20, at most three voices
    at once, and a rate limit so that Turbo does not become a buzz. The builder
    cannot hear, so it measures the levels in headless Chromium and reports
    them.

  of the SQL change without much conflict. You will be the first to actually
  hear it, so the levels and the choice of notes may need a round after it is
  live.

  The simulator's workload is now SQL through `internal/engine`, and the page

  **Branch:** `worktree-agent-a473ead9023e685db`, worktree

  Six commits, not pushed.

  **Step 0, measured (Apple M4, seed 7, five fake minutes at each age, against
  `main`):**
  - **Bounded read:** the engine runs `id > ?` on the key as a range scan, not a
  full scan with a filter. At 24 h a read still gives 100 rows while the table
  holds about 14,000.
  - **Cost by age:** it does not grow, so I went on building. Per fake minute
  with the page's settings:

  | | 0 h | 1 h | 6 h | 24 h |
  |---|---|---|---|---|
  | Native, ms | 16 | 29 | 27 | 35 |
  | Chromium, ms | 85 | 103 | 141 | 73 |
  | Chromium, `main`, ms | 52 | 20 | 35 | 25 |

    At Turbo a fake minute takes 12 real seconds, so this doesn't show.
  - **Cost against `main`:** about three times higher (1,440 steps a minute
  against 417), and the Go heap is 307 MB at 24 h against 151 MB. The engine
  yields between every two rows of a query, and each yield is a step of the
  scheduler. The page's comment sizing its 24 h stop at about 300 MB still
  holds. A smaller window would cut this; that's for you to decide.
  - **Position and outcome:** no engine change needed. The position comes from
  the engine's `statement.position` event, read by a logger handler keyed on the
  call's context. A write is `Conn.Begin`, insert, `Conn.Commit`, so a refusal
  at begin, a failed statement (`abandoned`), `unknown`
  (`core.ErrOutcomeUnknown`) and `not_committed` stay apart.

  **Decisions:**
  - **Table:** `create table orders (id integer primary key) strict, without
  rowid`. I didn't use `requests` because the page already uses "Requests" for
  app requests and the trace uses `request` for S3 requests.
  - **Statements:** `insert into orders (id) values (?)` and `select id from
  orders where id > ?`.
  - **Creating the table:** there is no `if not exists`. The first insert that
  fails with "no such table" runs the create and the insert again in the same
  transaction.
  - **The bound:** last key taken by the run's counter, minus the window, at
  least 0. It is taken when the call starts, so it is the same natively and in
  Chromium.
  - **`workload.keep`:** replaced by `workload.window` (1 to 10000, default
  100). No deletes remain anywhere.
  - **Read before the table exists:** reported as served, with no rows and the
  error `no such table: orders`, so the checker still checks it.

  **Self-review found one real hole, now fixed:** a stale read could slip past
  `fresh` whenever the newest window of keys all belonged to failed writes.
  Writes at or below the bound are now checked against the state at the read's
  position. The fast and slow checks now agree exactly, and a test runs every
  read of four keys both ways to show it.

  **Page:**
  - **Log lines** read like "a serves select id from orders where id > 812: 100
  rows." and "a commits insert into orders (id) values (913) as 3f6fbfd1."
  - **Commit objects** show each statement with its arguments.
  - **Counts:** the requests strip and the instance cards say inserts and
  selects.
  - **Intro:** says "each one SQL statement", and the "Built from" line links
  `internal/engine` next to `internal/core`.

  melody that wanders, and the same barcode always gets the same note. The notes
  are five to eight from the scale the other sounds use, moved up into a
  scanner's register of roughly 1.2 to 3 kHz.

❯ I think it makes sense that it depends on the written key, so two reads of    
  the same key get the same tone.                                                 ⎿  Message queued for delivery to a473ead9023e685db at its next tool 
     round. Sending this moved nothing it is running to the background just

⏺ Changed with the builder: the note now comes from the key, and inserts and
  selects share the mapping.

  - Insert: the scanner beep at the note of the key it writes.
  - Select: a select here returns up to 100 rows, not one key, so I made its
    quiet tick take the note of the newest key it returned. The reads after the
    write of key 31 echo that write's note until key 32 is written. A side
    effect I like: a read that is behind sounds like an older key.
  - No repeating run: the key goes through a small fixed scramble before it
    picks a note, so consecutive keys do not walk up the scale, and the same key
    gives the same note in every session.

  If you meant something else by a read's key, say so and I'll change it.

⏺ Yes to both, and I sent the builder one consolidated spec so the earlier
  corrections cannot get mixed up.

  - Keys are barcode numbers. I took your hash idea one step further: each key
    is a 13-digit number in the shape of an EAN-13 barcode, with a check digit,
    scrambled from the run's counter so every call still gets a unique one. They
    start with 57, the barcode prefix for Denmark. A write reads insert into 
    barcodes (code) values (5740391825563).
  - A read asks for one key: select code from barcodes where code = ?. Its tick
    has the note of that key, so two reads of the same barcode sound the same,
    and the scanner beep of the insert has it too.
  - Which key a read asks for: a good share ask for the newest acknowledged key,
    because that is what lets the checker catch a stale read. The rest ask for
    one of the recent keys, including keys whose write failed, where "0 rows" is
    the right answer.
  - Cost: a read now returns one row and not 100, so the cost per fake minute
    should fall back towards what it was before SQL. The builder measures it
    again.

  I asked the builder to say plainly if the checker can catch less with
  single-key reads than with the window.

✻ Worked for 1h 51m 47s · done Friday 2:18 PM

❯ I'd like the data to persist between refreshes (just like S3 would), with a   
  reset button available.                                                       

⏺ fabrik:builder(Builder: persistent bucket in simulator)
  ⎿  Backgrounded agent (↓ to manage · ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ A builder is started on it. What I told it to build:

  - What persists: the bucket only, in the browser's IndexedDB: the manifest and
    the commit objects. The instances' caches, processes, faults, and clock
    shifts are lost at a reload, as a machine would lose them.
  - After a reload: a starts on the existing bucket with an empty cache and gets
    its data from S3, and b stands by. If the real code waits for the old lease
    or takes time to restore, the page shows that and does not shortcut it.
  - Fake time continues across a reload, so the log and the commit objects read
    as one history.
  - Reset: a button in the playback bar that empties the stored bucket and
    starts from nothing, with no confirmation.
  - Two tabs: the first owns the stored bucket, and a second runs in memory
    only.
  - Old data: if the storage formats change, or the stored bucket is damaged,
    the page discards it and starts fresh with one log line.

  Open point the builder measures first: the log in the bucket now grows across
  visits, so it needs a cap. I suggested that the page stops at the cap and says
  that Reset starts over; the builder picks the number from how long an
  instance takes to open on a large bucket.

  I'll report when it is done.

✻ Worked for 1m 25s · done Friday 2:21 PM

❯ What do you mean two tabs?                                                    
⏺ If someone opens the simulator in two browser tabs at once, both would run
  their own session and both would save to the same stored bucket. Each tab has
  its own instances and its own in-memory S3, so they do not know of each other.
  Their commits would overwrite each other in storage, and the next reload
  would find a bucket that neither session produced, which the instances could
  not open.

  So the first tab gets the stored bucket, and a second tab runs as the page did
  before today: in memory, with nothing saved. It says so in one line.

  It is an edge case, and most visitors never hit it. The alternative is to let
  both tabs share one bucket for real, with leases deciding between them, which
  would be fun but is a much larger job.

✻ Sautéed for 3s · done Friday 2:22 PM

❯ Just use one bucket but different database prefixes per tab?                  
  ⎿  Message queued for delivery to a6701f8212f3ee0a6 at its next tool 
     round. Sending this moved nothing it is running to the background just
     now, so the message waits for that round unless something moves 
     sooner. If it finishes first, it is resumed with the message and 
     notifies again, so wait for that notification before resending.

⏺ Yes, that is better, and the builder has it now: one stored bucket, and each
  tab opens its own database under its own prefix.

  How a tab finds its prefix:

  1. Refresh: the tab remembers its prefix, so it gets its data back.
  2. Closed and came back later: a new tab takes over the most recent database
     that no open tab is using.
  3. A second tab at the same time: it starts its own empty database under a new
     prefix.

  Reset empties only the tab's own database. Databases left by closed tabs are
  deleted once a newer one has been taken over, so storage does not fill up with
  leftovers.

✻ Sautéed for 22s · done Friday 2:23 PM

❯ Maybe different tabs should just leave data from other tabs alone?            
                                                                                
  And give each tab a unique name of database, which we can then surface.       
  ⎿  Message queued for delivery to a6701f8212f3ee0a6 at its next tool 
     round. Moved 1 running tool call to the background without stopping 
     it, so that round no longer waits for it. If it finishes first, it is 
     resumed with the message and notifies again, so wait for that 
     notification before resending.

⏺ Changed with the builder. A tab is now a database:

  - Unique name per tab, short and readable like db-k3f9x2, shown with the
    bucket on the S3 node.
  - Refresh keeps the tab's database and its name.
  - A new tab always gets a new name and an empty database. It never adopts,
    reads, or deletes another tab's data, including a closed tab's.
  - Reset empties the tab's own database and keeps the name.

  Two consequences you should know:

  - Closing the tab ends the database for the visitor. Coming back tomorrow in a
    new tab starts an empty one. The data persists between refreshes, as you
    asked, but not between visits.
  - Leftovers: since nothing adopts or deletes a closed tab's database, each
    visit leaves one behind in the browser's storage. I added one exception as
    hygiene: a database that has not been written to for 30 days and that no
    open tab uses is removed. Say so if you want that gone too.

✻ Brewed for 28s · done Friday 2:33 PM

❯ Can we instruct the browser to just keep it as session storage, so we don't   
  have to do cleanup ourselves?                                                 
  ⎿  Message queued for delivery to a6701f8212f3ee0a6 at its next tool 
     round. Sending this moved nothing it is running to the background just
     now, so the message waits for that round unless something moves 
     sooner. If it finishes first, it is resumed with the message and 
     notifies again, so wait for that notification before resending.

⏺ Yes. The browser's session storage does exactly that: it survives a refresh,
  every new tab gets its own empty one, and the browser deletes it when the tab
  closes. The builder now uses it, and the locks and the 30-day cleanup are
  gone.

  Two things follow:

  - The limit is about 5 MB per tab. That sets the cap on the database. The
    builder measures how many fake hours fit, and the page stops cleanly before
    a write can fail, with a line that says Reset starts over.
  - A duplicated tab starts as a copy with the same database name, because the
    browser copies session storage when it duplicates a tab. The two then run
    independently. I told the builder not to build anything against that.

  The unique name per tab stays, shown with the bucket on the S3 node.

✻ Cooked for 27s · done Friday 2:35 PM

❯ Could we also name the instances uniquely? Then we could actually show if an  
  instance in a different tab but the same database has the lease etc.          

⏺ Naming the instances uniquely is easy. But the second half does not work with
  what we just chose: session storage is private to a tab, so two tabs never
  have the same database. Each has its own copy, and an instance in another tab
  can never hold the lease on yours.

  For that, the tabs would have to share one live bucket. I like it as a demo,
  since two tabs are two truly independent runtimes, and it is the most
  convincing way to show a lease. It is also the biggest change to the simulator
  since the live engine:

  - Shared storage: the bucket moves out of the tab into storage that all tabs
    see, so session storage is out and the question of leftovers is back. One
    shared database for the site with Reset would be the simple form.
  - Live requests: every S3 request of an instance has to reach the shared
    bucket as it happens, with conditional writes that are atomic across tabs.
    Today the bucket is in the tab's memory inside the fake-time bubble. I think
    the trick that lets a session wait for the visitor can carry this, but it
    has to be proven.
  - Clocks: each tab has its own fake clock, with its own pause and Turbo. A
    paused tab losing its lease to a running one is a good demo. A Turbo tab
    beside a 1× tab is a clock drift of five times, which leases do not promise
    to survive, so stale reads could show up that are no bug. Either the tabs
    share one clock, or the page has to say so.
  - Checker: it sees only its own tab's requests, so across tabs it can check
    the chain of commits and little else.

  My recommendation: let the builder finish persistence in session storage now,
  as you asked for it. Then, if you want it, shared tabs become their own piece
  of work, starting with a short experiment on the live requests, and the unique
  instance names come with it.

  Do you want shared tabs as that next piece?

✻ Cogitated for 50s · done Friday 2:38 PM

❯ Why can it sometimes fetch 0 rows? "6:05.4                                    
  a serves select code from barcodes where code = 5760050801136: 0 rows."       

⏺ That is by design, and 0 rows was the right answer there. Half of the selects
  ask for the newest barcode that was inserted. The other half pick one of the
  last 50 barcodes handed out, and a barcode is handed out when an insert
  starts, also when that insert then fails. Such a barcode never reached the
  table.

  An insert fails when the instance is down or crashed, when S3 hangs or is
  disconnected, or when requests go to the instance without the lease. So I
  expect an earlier line in your log where the insert of 5760050801136 failed.
  If there is none, tell me, because then it is a bug.

  The checker verifies every select against the log, also these, and the alarm
  would have sounded if the row should have been there.

  It is confusing as it reads now, though. Two ways to fix it:

  - Say why (my pick): the line reads something like "0 rows, its insert
    failed". It then shows that a failed insert really left nothing behind.
  - Never ask for them: selects only pick barcodes whose insert committed, so 0
    rows only appears if something is wrong.

  Which do you prefer? And the question on shared tabs from before is still
  open.

✻ Churned for 54m 0s · done Friday 3:14 PM

⏺ Agent "Builder: persistent bucket in simulator" is waiting on its own
  background work · 54m 21s

› Message from fabrik:builder
  The bucket now survives a reload, and every check passes. Branch
  `worktree-agent-a6701f8212f3ee0a6`, 7 commits, worktree
  `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.claude/worktrees/agent-a6701f8212f3ee0a6`.
  Not pushed. The diary is
  `docs/diary/2026-11-20-simulator-persistent-bucket.md`.

  **Step 0, numbers**
  - **Seeding:** `simstore` has no way to put an object in place. The engine
  writes each object through a client of its own (`seed`, which never crashes),
  retrying until `Store.Object` shows it, up to 100 tries. The store computes
  the versions itself. Writes are seen when the store traces them as applied.
  - **Commit objects:** 135 bytes each, manifest 312 bytes. The page's workload
  writes about 548 objects (74 KB) per fake hour.
  - **sessionStorage limits (measured):** Chromium allows 5,242,880 characters
  of any kind. WebKit allows 5,242,880 characters below 256, but only 2,621,440
  of others. I could not install Firefox through Playwright; MDN says 5 MiB. So
  I store one character per byte, not base64, which costs 4/3.
  - **Loading from sessionStorage:** 4 ms for 1,000 objects, 36 ms for 15,000.
  - **No compaction:** the core never snapshots, compacts or deletes commit
  objects.

  On an existing bucket, `a` waits about 33 s for the old holder's lease to run
  out. It then downloads every commit object one after another, each twice,
  about 0.13 s of fake time each:

  | Commit objects | `a` serves, fake time | Real time, native / Chromium |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | 108 | 48 s | 0.05 / 0.15 s |
  | 1,011 | 165 s | 0.8 / 1.0 s |
  | 5,002 | 675 s | 3.7 / 5.1 s |
  | 15,025 | 1,946 s | 12.7 / 20.9 s |

  **After a reload**, the clock and the commit times go on from where they were.
  The log says "S3 still holds db-xxxxxx: N commit objects", then "a takes the
  lease over", then "a catches up". For the 519 commit objects of one fake hour,
  `a` served about 1:40 fake minutes after the reload.

  **Cap:** 2,000 commit objects, about 3.6 fake hours of the page's workload. It
  is set by the wait after a reload (about 4.5 fake minutes), not by storage
  (7% of the quota). At the cap the session stops with "S3 is full. … Reset to
  start over."

  **What a reload can lose:** only the changes of the one command whose answer
  had not arrived, at most one step of play. What is stored is always the state
  after some earlier answer, because each answer's writes happen in one task
  with the metadata written last. If a write fails, the stored database is
  dropped and the page goes on in memory, with one log line.

  **Old or broken data:** a wrong version or a damaged bucket is refused by the
  engine. The page then drops it, starts empty and says so in one line. A
  failure of the start for another reason keeps the stored data.

  **Reads:** a select now asks only for an acknowledged barcode or one from the
  stored log. The checker gains: a read of a stored key at the empty log is now
  flagged as `fresh`; before, it passed every check. It loses nothing; "0 rows"
  is now always a violation.

  **Checks:** all pass: native tests (and `-race -count=20` on the new tests),
  `make check-browser`, `make test-browser`, `make lint`, `make site`, `make
  site-check`. The same seeded session gives the same hash natively and in
  Chromium. I compared that by hand; no test compares the two builds.

  **For you or Markus to decide:**
  - **Second-tab test:** `make site-check` cannot open a second tab, because its
  frames share one sessionStorage. It clears the storage to stand in for a new
  tab instead. I checked a real second tab by hand in Playwright: new name, no
  commit objects.
  - **Cap:** 2,000 means a wait of minutes after a reload. Raise or lower it?
  - **Phone layout:** at 390 px, Reset wraps the playback bar onto a second row.
  - **Seed failure:** if the store cannot take the bucket (only with faults set
  to 1), `start` is refused, and the session that ran before is already ended.

  Screenshots are in `/private/tmp/claude-502/-Users-maragubot-Developer-s4ql/68
  c0d47f-ada8-4603-995b-2482fe6fef3a/scratchpad/shots/`:

⏺ Persistence is built, with every check passing. The builder is merging in the
  Turbo change, and then it goes to its pull request and live.

  - Refresh: the bucket, the fake time, and the commit objects' times carry on.
    The log starts with "S3 still holds db-bjdpwb: 402 commit objects."
  - Name: shown on the S3 node as s4ql-sim/db-bjdpwb/.
  - Reset: a button beside Sound. On a phone it wraps the playback bar onto a

    a violation.

  The refresh shows two things about the real code, which is what the simulator
  is for:

  - a waits about 33 s for the old holder's lease to run out before it takes
    over.
  - a then downloads every commit object one after another, each one twice, at
    about 0.13 s of fake time each. The core has no snapshot or compaction, so
    opening on an existing bucket takes time in proportion to its whole history:
    48 s for 108 commits, 165 s for 1,011, and 32 minutes for 15,025.

  Because of that, the builder set the cap at 2,000 commit objects, about 3.6
  fake hours. A refresh at the cap means about 4.5 fake minutes before a serves,
  or just under a minute of real time at Turbo. Storage is not the limit: 2,000
  objects use 7% of the quota. At the cap the session stops with "S3 is full. …
  Reset to start over."

  The double download looks like something to fix in the core. I have not looked
  into why it happens. Do you want me to have that checked?

⏺ A builder is on it. To be exact about the numbers: a normal run of the job
  takes about 14 minutes, and the 30 was the job's timeout after a hang. The
  builder works on both:

  - Normal run: the target is about 5 minutes, without dropping or weakening any
    test. It measures where the 14 minutes go first, then splits the packages
    over parallel jobs, caches the wasm build and Chromium, and removes waste in
    the runner.

    and the test, without blocking the others. It also looks for the cause of
    today's hang.

› Message from fabrik:builder
  Hang: found where it is, and a fix that is in CI now. Not CPU starvation and
  not the tests: the first Chromium started on a fresh runner VM sometimes never
  requests the page at all. Evidence from draft PR #98 (branch
  faster-browser-ci), where the runner now reports each step: in 26
  single-Chromium jobs (one Chromium per VM, nothing else running), 5 first
  starts hung ("chromium started after 71ms; no output from the program", no
  "the page loaded" step), and Chromium's log shows D-Bus calls failing for 20+
  s and "Network service crashed or was terminated, restarting service". Extra
  flags (--disable-gpu --disable-dev-shm-usage --password-store=basic
  --use-mock-keychain) did not help (1 hang in 8 with, 1 in 8 without). A
  Chromium started again on the same VM worked every time (once it took 3
  attempts). In the old job, the four first-batch packages are the four
  Chromiums started at the VM's first minute, which is why the same four hang
  together and the three later ones pass.

  Fix, in one commit of its own on top of main: 786c981 "Make the browser runner
  start a Chromium again that does not start the program..."
  (internal/sqlite/browser/main.go, main_test.go, worker.js; no Makefile or
  workflow change). The runner restarts a Chromium that has not started the
  program within 1 minute (or that exits before it), up to 3 attempts, writing
  what it saw each time; it also stops Chromium 30 s after -test.timeout (before
  go test kills it) and on SIGINT/SIGTERM/SIGQUIT, and names the tests running
  under -test.v. So a start hang costs about a minute instead of 30. It can be
  cherry-picked onto main now; `go test -race ./internal/sqlite/browser/` covers
  it with a fake Chromium. I have not yet run the full code-review on it. Next:
  shorten the start-up detection (time to the page load), then the speed-up.

⏺ The builder found the hang, and my guess was wrong. It is not load and not our
  tests: the first Chromium started on a fresh CI machine sometimes never loads
  the page at all.

  - Evidence: in 26 jobs that each started one Chromium on a fresh machine with
    nothing else running, 5 hung at the first start. Chromium's own log shows
    its D-Bus calls failing for over 20 s and its network service crashing. A
    Chromium started again on the same machine worked every time.
  - Why the same four packages: they are the four Chromiums started in the
    machine's first minute. The three later ones pass.
  - So #96 was unlucky twice, and its changes are not the cause.

  The fix is in the test runner: if Chromium has not started the program within
  a minute, the runner starts it again, up to three times, and says what it saw.
  A bad start then costs about a minute, not 30.

  A sub-agent is merging that fix to main as its own pull request now, and then
  brings #96 up to date and merges it. The builder goes on with the speed-up of
  the normal run.

✻ Baked for 4s · done Friday 5:14 PM

› Message from fabrik:builder
  Branch `worktree-agent-ab962a50c1acfc5e6`, worktree
  /Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.claude/worktrees/agent-ab962a50c1acfc5e6,
  based on a merge of `origin/worktree-agent-a6701f8212f3ee0a6` (#96). Five
  commits, nothing pushed.

  **Shares:** the new setting `workload.deletes` (0 to 100) defaults to 25, so a
  quarter of writes are deletes. With the default 40 % writes, calls are 30 %
  insert, 10 % delete and 60 % select. One select in eight asks for one of the
  last 10 acknowledged deletes, and the event's `expect` says `row` or `none`.

  **Checker:** a write is `txn{key, delete}`. A key is inserted once and deleted
  by at most one transaction: a key a delete has picked is never picked again,
  and an unknown outcome leaves it in limbo. That is how the checker tells a
  delete from the insert of the same key. For every write, `fresh` now goes by
  the read's position, and the fast and slow paths agree (the exhaustive test
  also covers deletes). New constructed-trace tests catch each case you listed,
  among others. Stored shape raised to 2.

  **Cost:** steps per fake minute are 431 at 0.5 h and 433 at the cap (2,324
  commit objects), against 425 and 431 before. Wall time was the same within
  noise from a loaded machine (median about 27 ms). Hash `a35f8195…` is the same
  natively and in Chromium.

  **Sound:** the delete is a void tick, the key's note then an octave drop, at
  −38.0 dBFS. A scan is −36.3 and a select −42.0. The burst peaks at −26.0 dBFS,
  and −21.7 with the alarm, both within limits.

  **Screenshots:** in /private/tmp/claude-502/-Users-maragubot-Developer-s4ql/68
  c0d47f-ada8-4603-995b-2482fe6fef3a/scratchpad/delete-builder/:

❯ Our design is kinda Ryoji Ikeda.                                              

⏺ Stop Task
  ⎿  Error: No task found with ID: a42bfccade7a28a87

⏺ It is, and more so since today. His "test pattern" turns data into
  black-and-white barcode strips with sound made from the same data, and
  "datamatics" does it with tones and clicks. The simulator now does the small
  version of that: each commit is drawn as a barcode and beeps at a pitch taken
  from its key, in white on black, with square waves.

  The difference is that his data is the material, and ours is a real system
  doing work. That is a nice angle if you ever describe the page: the pattern
  and the sound are not decoration, they are the storage engine's actual
  commits.

  If you want to lean into it, the two cheapest steps would be the bar field in
  the playback bar pulsing with each commit in time with its beep, and a
  full-screen mode that shows only the barcodes and plays the sound. Neither is
  started; say so if you want one.

✻ Worked for 2s · done Friday 10:39 PM

❯ I'd also like someone looking at the simulator to try out the storage engine. 
  Maybe a small SQL box and a results table below it? Independent of the rest   
  of the simulator perhaps. Thoughts?                                           
  ⎿  1 skill available

⏺ I'd build it, but inside the simulator, not independent of it.

  An independent box would be an SQL prompt with less SQL than SQLite has, and
  nothing to see behind it. What is worth trying is the storage: you type an
  insert, its commit object appears in S3 as a barcode with your statement on
  it, you crash a, refresh the page, and select the row back. That only works if
  your statement is a request in the same session, to the instance that
  requests go to, under the faults you have set.

  How I would do it:

  - The box: one statement, a Run button, and the result as a table below it, or
    the engine's error as it words it.
  - Same path as the app's requests: your statement shows in the log, commits as
    its own object, beeps, and fails or waits when the instance is down or S3
    hangs.
  - Your own tables: you can create, insert, update, delete, and drop tables of
    your own. The app's barcodes table is read-only for the box, so a stray
    delete from barcodes cannot make the checker and the app's requests go
    wrong. You can still select from it.
  - While paused: Run steps fake time just far enough for the answer. If S3
    hangs, the statement waits until you play.
  - Limits to design for: there is no limit or order by yet, so select * from 
    barcodes can return over a thousand rows; the table would show the first 50
    or so and say how many more there are. A line under the box links to the
    roadmap for what SQL works.
  - Persistence: your tables survive a refresh with the rest of the bucket, and
    count toward the cap of 2,000 commit objects.

  The cost of this over an independent box is the read-only rule and the checker
  having to accept your commits in the log. The gain is that it shows the thing
  the page is about.

  Do you want it integrated like this, or do you still prefer it independent?
